A.20. Executing Entity information

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
Somalia is one of the most fragile countries in the world from various points of view, including security, inequality, displacement, conflicts over natural resources, and economic development, which are exacerbated due to climate change. The country has a population of about 13 million people, with an estimated 20% living below the poverty line (World Bank, 2020). According to the UNICEF’s Rapid Population Health Survey (RPHS) (classification notes that 1.7 million children below the age of 5 face acute malnutrition. The country is also facing a food security crisis with an estimated 3.4 million people in need of humanitarian assistance, including food aid. The agricultural sector in Somalia accounts for approximately 75% of gross domestic product (GDP) and employs about 80% of the population. However, the agricultural production system in Somalia is highly vulnerable to climate change, which depends on natural pastures on the country's vast rangelands. Agricultural productivity is very low due to high levels of soil degradation and poor water management practices. The rainfall pattern in Somalia is characterized by a dry season where the average annual rainfall is about 200 mm in most parts of the country. The northern coasts receive slightly less annual rainfall (only up to 50 mm) while the south receives approximately 400 mm (Somalia NAPA, 2013).


---
**Page 7**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

There have been significant changes in the climate and extreme climatic events in Somalia since the baseline period (1981-2010) and climate change is increasing the likelihood of compounding and cascading impacts for communities and ecosystems. The frequency, intensity, duration, and spatial extent of droughts are expected to increase across the country in the near, mid-, and far futures under different scenarios (RCP 2 and RCP 6.5). Coastal areas can see increased precipitation with more intense rainfall events, while inland areas will experience less frequent but more extreme rainfall events is projected to increase across the country. The frequency and intensity of droughts have been rising and are expected to continue increasing in the future, leading to a decline in water availability for livestock and humans. Floods during the Gu season are also expected to increase in certain regions, posing risks to populations due to lack of infrastructure.

Depleted technical capacity in government institutions due to years of conflict, low investment in agriculture, and inadequate access to climate information among last-mile users are significant barriers that must be lifted for local communities to adapt effectively. The project will address these barriers. It restores productive landscapes so they can sustain resilient livelihoods, promoting sustainable land management practices and climate-resilient agricultural systems, increasing water availability (through improved water harvesting, storage, and use), reducing soil erosion, improving market access for smallholders, and strengthening institutional frameworks and coordination for landscape restoration.

The project is premised on the following theory of change: If local communities' capacity is strengthened to implement sustainable land management practices and climate-resilient agricultural systems in their landscapes, and if this capacity is improved, then the resilience of local communities and ecosystems to climate change will be enhanced, because these practices are designed to increase water availability for livestock and humans, reduce soil erosion, and improve productivity.

The project partners that work together create a context in which, on the one hand, communities participate in the restoration of the productive landscape, and, on the other hand, they are supported by adequate enabling environment. The project will build on lessons learned from previous projects and programs and is closely coordinated with other efforts to rebuild the country following years of conflict.

The project will benefit a total of 124,836 people (1,152,142 direct beneficiaries and 972,689 indirect beneficiaries). The project will be implemented in four districts: Baidoa (USP-1C), Burao (USP-1D), Togdheer (USP-T1) by supporting the country's adaptation and mitigation goal, Tog Fisid (USP-0529 beneficiary adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices), and Hiran District (USP-0486 beneficiary adopting climate-resilient agricultural management) through TCG locally-led adaptation approach. The Project will also support dissemination of climate information to smallholder farmers in the project area, including those who are not directly targeted by TCG (by supporting market chains).

### Outcome 1: Restored landscapes are resilient and sustainably managed.

Activities under this Outcome aim is designed to restore productive landscapes and facilitate ecological resilience, supporting ecosystem adaptation to climate change so that they can provide crucial ecosystem services and sustain the livelihoods of local communities. The project will focus on restoring degraded lands through sustainable land management systems and the deployment of urgent landscape restoration interventions. Given the importance of water as a limiting factor for agricultural production in the sub-watershed at a planning unit.

### Outcome 2: Local livelihoods are resilient to climate change.

Activities under this outcome are designed to ensure that local communities derive increased and improved climate resilience from their land management practices (e.g., agroforestry, crop diversification, and livestock grazing) which are climate-vulnerable staple crops, as well as livestock production. In these value chains, the project also supports the transition of unsustainable, unproductive, and maladapted production systems with low productivity to more sustainable, productive, and resilient production systems (Els, Oromia). It will also support market infrastructure solutions at both landscape and term level.

### Outcome 3: Strengthening institutional frameworks for sustainable land management and climate-resilient agriculture is in place at State and Federal Levels

Activities under Outcome 3 form part of the project's sustainability and upscaling strategy. The aim is to create conditions that will enable the Government of Somalia, particularly the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, to address the fragile conditions of the Government of Somalia; strengthening of capacity is required to ensure that Federal Member States can implement new norms for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture.


---
**Page 8**
---

The activities will enhance the institutional capacity of the Government of Somalia at various levels. Output 3.1 considers legal frameworks, implementation monitoring modalities and coordination (the normative elements of institutional capacity) as well as the ability to generate and disseminate useful, relevant and timely climate information in particular its capacity to generate and disseminate useful, relevant and timely climate information to last mile users.

The project will be executed by FAO and the Government of Somalia acting through (i) the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change (MECC) and (ii) the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MAAI) in a co-execution modality to deliver the project activities.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### B.1.1 Climate context

Development problem of Somalia: High vulnerability and food insecurity

- **1.** Somalia is the most fragile country in the world[1] from various points of view, including security, inequality, drought, and climate change.
- **2.** The country has experienced a prolonged period of conflict since 1980, which has exacerbated its vulnerability to climate change. Somalia has already experienced impacts of climate change on people's livelihoods due to limited adaptive capacity and high exposure to the effects of climate change[2].
- **3.** Somalia is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world and one of the least able to face the climate crisis.
- **4.** 6.6 million people or 39% of the total population currently have high levels of acute food insecurity according to the 2021 IPC (Integrated Food Security Phase Classification) report[3]. The country has experienced a prolonged period of conflict since 1980, which has exacerbated its vulnerability to climate change. Somalia has already experienced impacts of climate change on people's livelihoods due to limited adaptive capacity and high exposure to the effects of climate change.
- **5.** Somalia is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world and one of the least able to face the climate crisis.

#### B.1.2 Socio-economic context

The agricultural sector in Somalia accounts for approximately 75% of gross domestic product (GDP)[4], with 65% of the population depending on agriculture, livestock production, and fishing as their main livelihoods[5]. The majority of the rural population lives in semi-arid pastoral areas. The country has a total land area of about 637,000 km², of which approximately 400,000 km² is arable land (only up to 50 m) while the rest receives approximately 400 mm (Somalia NAPA, 2013).

#### B.1.3 Environmental degradation

Environmental degradation in Somalia is a complex phenomenon that results from both natural and anthropogenic causes[6]. Approximately 27.5% of the land area in Somalia is environmentally degraded due to climatic and ecological needs. The changes in land use patterns have considerably changed, leading to soil erosion and desertification. These changes have contributed to the present state of land degradation in Somalia.

#### B.1.4 Inadequate water management

Inadequate water management activities, non-regulated charcoal production for local consumption and export, uncontrolled grazing of livestock, deforestation, overgrazing, lack of proper waste disposal systems, poor agricultural practices, and lack of conservation measures have accelerated environmental degradation in some areas (SWALIM, LACO, 2018).

#### B.1.5 Inadequate water management

Inadequate water management negatively impacts the social and economic resilience to climate change, livestock and livelihoods of displaced and host communities. In places with healthy biodiversity, Prosopis spp. (Prosopis) still offer a good source of firewood and charcoal[7]. However, in areas where the natural vegetation has been replaced by hydroponic are cangeled rangelands and forest areas and one of the problems causing decreased availability of water resources is the overgrazing of livestock which leads to soil erosion. Other causes include deforestation including in marine areas by invading arable land and thereby reducing the area available for cropping use and increasing the risk of desertification.

#### B.1.6 Weak governance

Weak governance, lack of infrastructure development, and poor institutional capacity are some of the factors that have contributed to the current state of environmental degradation in Somalia[8]. The weak governance is one of the biggest challenges for communities to stabilize agricultural productivity and livelihoods. Inadequate water management activities, non-regulated charcoal production for local consumption and export, uncontrolled grazing of livestock, deforestation, overgrazing, lack of proper waste disposal systems, poor agricultural practices, and lack of conservation measures have accelerated environmental degradation in some areas (SWALIM, LACO, 2018).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Climate-Development Nexus: Climate Drivers and Climate Change Impacts

8. There have been significant changes in the climate and extreme climatic events in Somalia during the baseline period (1975–2004). These changes are projected to continue, with major impacts on communities and ecosystems. Maximum temperatures have shown significantly large increase at the rate of 0.63°C per decade for the entire country, while minimum temperatures increased by 0.48°C per decade in Lower Juba, Lower Shebelle, and Bay regions, which receive higher annual precipitation, have experienced a 29% reduction in rainfall since 1975 (1975–2004). The number of days with maximum temperature above 32°C (daily) is variably significant; an increase is observed in the Bar, Lower Juba, and Gedo regions. There is also a significant increase in the number of hot nights (when minimum temperatures are greater than or equal to 25.5–26.0°C) for the entire country. An increase in the number of very hot days (≥37°C) is significant in coastal areas of Somaliland, Lower Juba, and Bay regions. The number of cold nights (when minimum temperatures are less than or equal to 14°C) has decreased significantly; this decrease is observed in the Bar, Lower Juba, and Gedo regions. An increase in the number of very cold nights (when minimum temperatures are below 5°C) is significant in coastal areas of Somaliland.

9. These climate changes are projected to continue. Both mean maximum and minimum temperatures are projected to increase under both RCP 8.5 and RCP 6.0 for the years 2010–2039, mid (2040–2059) and far (2070–2099) futures (based on the latest climate model projections). The projected increase in mean maximum temperatures over this period is reached in the far future (2070–2099) in RCP 8.5 (4°C). The number of days with maximum temperatures

## B

### 10. The frequency of agricultural drought events is projected to increase in future decades.

The frequency and severity of droughts are expected to increase, with the percentage of cropland and number of days affected by drought increasing over time. The frequency during the cu season (March-June) has increased since the mid-2000s, while in the dry season (October-December), it is projected to remain relatively similar between 2010–2011 and 2050–2069. However, agricultural drought during the September-December season is projected to remain relatively similar under both scenarios.

### 11. Floods from the cu season are projected to increase in Lower Luwba, Mubug, Middle Luwba and together. The high interannual variability points to high uncertainty in future projections.

Floods from the cu season are projected to increase in Lower Luwba, Mubug, Middle Luwba and together. The high interannual variability points to high uncertainty in future projections.

### 12. Cyclones and storms have caused severe impacts and risks. Somalia has experienced at least one major climate extreme event per decade since the mid-20th century. In 2020, Somalia experienced its wettest year ever since 2000 (UNCCD, EMDAT). Floods are the most frequent events experienced in Somalia. Somalia is also vulnerable to cyclones and storms. Cyclones and storms constituted the remaining 11%. Most of the flooding is due to overflow of the Shebelle and Juba rivers because of heavy rainfall and snowmelt from the Ethiopian highlands. In 2023, there was an upsurge of reported open breaches along the Juba and Shabelle rivers.

### 13. The economic cost of droughts and floods in Somalia is high. For example, the 2010–2011 drought, which triggered a famine, resulted in 657,000 people requiring food assistance at its peak. The total estimated cost was US$ 2.23 billion and USD 1.02 billion respectively. Losses associated with four main staple crops (maize, sorghum, millet, and wheat) were estimated to be US$ 875 million in income lost for pastoralists and agro-pastoralists. Over 2.2 million people were internally displaced within the country due to droughts and floods between August 2021 and May 2023. In 2020, flooding resulted in the deployment of nearly 342,000 people, inundating 294 villages and destroying over 57,000 houses. The cost to Somalia’s economy was estimated at US$ 81 million. The economic cost of floods during Deyr (October-December) season, at least 1.5 million ha of farmland was swamped causing 20% loss in crop production and an estimated US$ 347 million in losses to the agricultural sector between August 2022 and May 2024. Stored food was either inundated or washed away leading to food losses and consumption of emergency food aid.

### 14. Crop yields, which are always low, are likely to decline under climate change. Under climate change, maize yield is projected to decrease by up to 53% in Lower Luwba, Mubug, Middle Luwba and together. Projected yield growth under rainfed conditions for Shabelle and lower Shebelle regions under RCP 6.0.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Climate Change and Its Impacts on Livelihoods in Somalia

### 15. The average annual Temperature Humidity Index (THI), which is an indicator of heat stress on livestock, is already severe in the extreme emergency levels and is expected to further increase due to the increasing temperature. Heat stress has a direct impact on animal productivity and milk production.

### 16. Climate change will cause increased pressure on the already scarce fresh water resources, which could amplify competition between pastoralists and other users of water resources such as agriculture, industry, and urban areas for limited and water resources available for livestock, affecting communities in multiple ways. During droughts, the productivity of herds often declines due to reduced feed availability and heat stress. This results in increased mortality rates among livestock sales distress. The reduction of productivity and distress sales lead to the deterioration of terms of trade by increasing prices and reducing demand.

### 17. The SomaSociety is clan-oriented and competition among clans underlines natural resource management between farmers and pastoralists, which can be exacerbated during droughts when access to water resources becomes more scarce. However, these movements give rise to competition between livestock leading to conflicts. It allows for the spread of diseases such as anthrax, foot-and-mouth disease, and Rift Valley Fever. The lack of alternative food sources usually lead to pastoralists displace who in turn move to urban areas or search of other livelihood options.

### Capacity to anticipate and respond to climate change

#### 18. The current capacity of the livestock sector is limited by the age-old and the current coping strategies in response to extreme weather events on short-term solutions such as selling of assets. The limited adaptation capacity is due to a combination of factors including lack of access to information, extension services, and other resources such as credit, inputs, information, markets, and services. In large part these drive individual factors, including low adoption of climate change mitigation practices (e.g., improved breeds, drought-resistant varieties, agroforestry, water harvesting techniques, afforestation), and other livelihoods practices such as land use methods, especially among women and other vulnerable groups.

#### 19. Climate change adaptation is not well planned and does not address the underlying causes of environmental degradation, loss of indigenous species and increase of invasive species which negatively affect the social economic conditions in pastoral communities. The livestock sector has been affected by climate change with a significant crop loss occurred between 2008 and 2019[1]. Biological degradation is the highest type of land degradation (36%) followed by soil erosion (25%), desertification (17%), and salinization (14%). The livestock sector has been affected by invasive species. The Shabelle and Juba basins, vital areas for productivity, face heightened degradation risks due to overgrazing, deforestation, and the spread of invasive species such as Prosopis juliflora and Euphorbia tirucalli. These drivers are encouraged by the prevailing communal land tenure practices, poor governance, and civil war.[2]

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Limitations of access to timely and accurate climate information further hinder Somalia's ability to anticipate and prepare for floods and droughts; hampering early warning and response efforts.

The observation network coverage is below recommended levels, with only 12 weather stations installed and maintained (2 Automated Weather Stations (AWS), 10 manual rainfall stations, 5 synoptic stations and 7 Tipping Bucket Rain Gauges). The country has a limited number of climate monitoring systems in place. While the need for further development remains high, efforts are in place to strengthen and expand existing systems, however early warning and response capacity is still weak due to lack of trained personnel, limited access to information technology and agro-climate advice is not widely disseminated. Low rates of literacy also prevent many farmers from understanding weather forecasts.

In the last decade, Somalia has made strides in the establishment of political, social, environmental, and economic systems that are conducive to climate change adaptation. The country has developed a National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), NDC implementation plan and Somalia National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA). Refer to Annex B for more details.

The current investment at country level focuses more on humanitarian actions. USD 5.4 billion has been spent on life-saving emergency responses since the 2011 famine. However, it is estimated that building resilience in Somalia would deliver a much higher return on investment. For example, every USD invested in climate-smart agriculture and resilience measures yields average benefits of USD 28 for every USD invested*. Recent FAO reports have demonstrated that investments in climate-resilient agricultural practices can yield up to five times the benefit as compared with conventional farming methods. For example, farm-level DRR good practices perform on average 2.2 times better than usual practices under conditions of drought and flooding. Every USD I invested in anticipatory action can yield up to USD 7 in benefits and avoided agricultural losses.

## Targeted areas

The targeted areas of the project were pre-defined based on the climate risk analysis*, which considered climate hazard, exposure, vulnerability and adaptive capacity, and the agro-ecological zones in Somalia (details in Annex). The targeted areas are: Lower Juba region (LJ); Jubaland; Middle Shabelle region (MS); North Eastern region (NER); South West region (SW); Puntland; and the Somali Region. These areas include parts of the following sub-regions: Lower Juba region (LJ) (Hirshabele, Middle Shabelle region (MS)) (Jubaland), Lower Juba region, and other areas.

*Bebb, C., Wani, S.P., Kari, V.E. et al. Identifying hot spots of vulnerability associated with climate change in Africa: Climate Change 134, 717–731 (2014). https://doi.org/10.1051/climate/2014062

*FAO, The State of Food Insecurity in the World 2020: Building climate resilience and sustainable food systems to end hunger. <https://www.fao.org/state-of-food-insecurity-in-the-world/en/>

These regions were selected in close consultation with the government considering the government’s priority areas that have been newly liberated from armed groups in North Eastern, Jubaland and South West States where access to humanitarian assistance has been difficult and there are huge needs for support in response to climate change. 27. There has been a concerted push towards greater inclusivity and dialogue in Somalia. Inter-regional relations have seen some progress, with the establishment of the IGAD-Somalia Peace and Security Commission (PSC) developing alliances and partnerships to address common challenges. Security enhancements, including successful counter-terrorism operations by the Somali National Army (SNA), have contributed to a more stable environment. Economic growth and development initiatives have helped alleviate disparities between regions, showing that there is potential for greater regional cooperation in addressing ongoing and future climate challenges, will support this positive movement towards resilience and peace building.

## 30. Other project links and linkages with project design

The SRLMIL program provides high quality water and land information, crucial to relief, rehabilitation, and development initiatives in Somalia, including the implementation of the Climate Resilience Project (CRP) under the Green Climate Fund (GCF), as outlined in Outcomes 2 and 3.

The Climate Resilience Program ("Climate Adaptation for resilient livelihoods" and "Climate-resilient livelihoods to Food production and Nutrition outcomes (CLIMB)") are to improve food production and contribute to the resilience of food systems in Somalia. The project is implemented by the World Bank, with co-financing from GCF, and will be carried out through a consortium led by ICRISAT.

The Climate Resilience Project (CRP) is an ongoing project funded by the World Bank (WBG), including Kenya, Somalia, Ethiopia, and an intergovernmental entity (IGE). The project costs amount to US$65 Million. The development objective of the project is to enhance climate resilience in rural households through improved access to water resources, agricultural inputs, and services, as well as improved infrastructure.

### Linkages to other GCF projects

1. African Development Bank (AfDB) is developing a GCF country program titled "Program to Build Resilience and Adaptation to Climate Change in the Horn of Africa" with Somalia one of the participating countries. The AfDB project, which is under development, will operate in transboundary areas between Somalia and Ethiopia. There will be no geographical overlap or duplication with the SRLMIL project.
2. For further information on the GCF country program, please refer to Annex 6: FS. Climate Learning lessons from these projects and, in particular from AfDB's programming over the years in Somalia, have informed this project directly. The lessons learned include the importance of building local capacity for climate change adaptation through capacity building at community level with supportive interventions at landscape levels, while supporting the renewal of land tenure systems. In addition to the lessons learned on the importance of building local capacity and the approaches and technologies included in this project, they have done it from a solely socio-economic perspective; few have considered the potential for or risk from conflict. The project has therefore been designed with the aim to address the potential for, or risk from, conflict that climate vulnerability is a potential driver of conflict and must be addressed as such.

### B (2) (a). This theme addresses impacts of climate change on vulnerable people in 6 regions of Somalia which depend on agriculture and natural resources for their livelihoods. The project objective is to reduce the climate vulnerability of Somali rural communities by improving access to water, land information, and agricultural inputs, as well as reducing increased frequency and intensity of drought and floods. The project is premised on the following theory of change:

* User groups will have improved access to water resources, agricultural inputs, and services.
* Improved infrastructure will be in place for better management of natural resources.
* A resilient rural community will be established through the development of a landscape-level, integrated, climate-resilient agriculture and an institutional enabling environment is improved. THEN: The resilience of local communities across landscapes will increase as they are able to adapt their livelihoods to changing climatic conditions using better managed landscapes and adapted climate resilient agriculture and supported by adequate enabling environment.

To achieve its objective, the project must create a series of interconnected targets:

* Building landscape-level capacity for climate-resilient natural resources management at the landscape level.

## Effective land management is also hindered by unclear land tenure rights and land ownership, frequent disputes, and the absence of a widely recognized and enforced legal system for land registration. There are also due to the weakness of local institutions.

### Due to the clan-based social structure in Somalia, land use planning must consider local dynamics and conflicts. Effective engagement with communities is necessary to ensure that land uses plans are acceptable and beneficial to local populations. The lack of a formalized land tenure system has led to frequent disputes over land ownership for public participation. A patriarchal culture prevails in Somalia, and women are often excluded from political decision-making processes.

### Despite the challenges, there have been some positive developments in land management in Somalia. International organizations and governmental bodies are working to build local capacities, develop better governance structures, and promote sustainable land use practices. However, these efforts need to be scaled up significantly. More concerted efforts will still be needed to create a robust framework for land use planning that can support Somalia’s national development goals.

### The integration of traditional pastoralist knowledge with modern approaches in landscape-level natural resource management approaches will be crucial, both as a way of promoting climate-resilient landscapes and as an approach to sustainable land use. This will require the involvement of all stakeholders, including local communities, government agencies, and international organizations.

## Barrier 2: Local communities do not yet have the technical or institutional capacity to fully participate in the management and allocation of natural resources, particularly water and land, which are in scarce supply already. Local communities’ traditional practices for managing these resources are often based on trial-and-error development. Poverty, conflict and food insecurity have led local communities towards short-term, mismanaged, unsustainable use of their resources. These practices are characterized by a lack of planning and decision-making that is predominantly guided by customary laws and traditions.

### In many pastoralist communities in Somalia, women play an important role in the management of natural resources such as water points and grazing areas which are managed through traditional systems. However, these systems often do not provide adequate protection for women’s rights allowing pastoralists to traverse large areas in search of pasture and water during following seasonal patterns, which is vital in an era of climate change.

### However, these traditional practices are increasingly misaligned to current and future climate conditions. Degraded and climate vulnerable landscapes fail to provide the basic ecological services—whether produce or protection, or both—that they once did. This results in a loss of biodiversity, reduced water quality, and increased soil erosion, especially water infiltration. Furthermore, educational attainment among women is very low. Overall, 75% of women are illiterate compared with only 20% for men. Women’s lack of education limits their ability to participate in traditional top-down literacy-based extension services, that tend to be oriented towards men due to land ownership and decision-making structures.

### In response to these challenges, there is a gradual push to integrate traditional management practices with more modern approaches. This will require the development of new technologies and innovations that are sensitive to environmental stewardship that aligns both with traditional and modern management cultures requires long term efforts. Women’s participation in land use planning and decision-making, which play an integral role in agriculture can also become agents of change.

## Barrier 3: There has so far been insufficient dissemination of technology on climate resilience practices and limited access to information services for pastoralists who are the most vulnerable group that could make their livelihoods more resilient, and do not have access to climate information services. While it is known that a transition towards more sustainable land use practices can help communities adapt to climate change and support development, there has yet been any significant effort to assist local population in effecting this transition. Most poor households are small-scale farmers who lack the resources for mechanization and rely on traditional methods of mechanization or reliance on natural rainfall limits crop yields. Access to modern irrigation systems is scarce. This lack of access to technology and information services also contributes to limited access to markets, credit, and extension services.

### Additionally, the limited access to agricultural processing facilities and effective post-harvest storage solutions exacerbates food insecurity in pastoralist communities. The lack of infrastructure for livestock transportation and transpiration, reducing the ability of farmers to sell their crops at top prices and further invest in improving their agricultural practices.

## Similarly, pastoralist households face significant challenges due to unsustainable grazing practices and limited access to water points. These challenges are exacerbated by climate change which has led to increased aridity and limited land resources, which contributes to land degradation and reduces the land’s long-term viability for supporting livestock. The scarcity of water points further exacerbates these challenges as pastoralists have to travel long

## Barrier B: The government's support to natural resources planning and climate adaptation is limited by its weak institutional capacity for land use planning and management, as well as the lack of reliable data on land ownership and land cover availability in all States.

### Records of land ownership and usage are incomplete and often outdated, making it difficult to enforce land use policies and dispute resolution. In addition, the legal frameworks governing land registration and property rights are weak and inconsistent across States. The government lacks a comprehensive national land information system (GIS) and land surveying, which are essential for modern land management practices.

### The lack of reliable data on land ownership and usage is compounded by the absence of up-to-date cadastral maps and other land information systems in many States.

## B

### SWALIM (Sudan Water and Land Information Management)
These systems help monitor hydrological data but need further expansion and enhancement to cover more areas and provide more precise forecasts.

#### Barrier 6: Lack of Climate Information Services impedes climate resilient agriculture.
Access to climate information services is critical for farmers, pastoralists, and other vulnerable groups in order to mitigate the impacts of climate variability and change. Despite the critical need for accurate climate forecasts, early warning systems, and agricultural advisories, these services are not widely available or accessible due to a number of factors. These include underdeveloped infrastructure, a lack of necessary hydro-eco-climate information and material capacity to gather, analyze, and disseminate this information in a timely manner; the absence of an enabling policy environment for the development of a strong, centralized government for long periods has made it difficult to implement coordinated, nationwide programs. In addition, there is a lack of adequate funding for climate services, which limits the ability of governments to facilitate the wider distribution of climate information. Financial resources for climate services have been limited investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as competition with other development priorities resulting from ongoing conflicts and recurring natural disasters.

#### The barriers to access to climate information services are:
- **Barriers 1**: Restricted access to climate information.
- **Barriers 2**: Lack of capacity for the effective use of climate information.
- **Barriers 3**: Limited dissemination of climate information.
- **Barriers 4**: Inadequate funding for climate services.

### Proposed Climate Solutions
The proposed project addresses these climate challenges by working directly with crop and livestock producers to improve agricultural production and ensure the resilience of livelihoods. The project adopts an integrated approach that seeks to increase the productivity, profitability, and sustainability of smallholder agriculture through more stable and increased yields, leading to improved adaptive capacity of the most vulnerable people.

| Outputs | Project Activities |
| --- | --- |
| 1.1 National climate change adaptation strategy | 2.1 Strengthen national climate change adaptation strategies and policies |
| 1.2 Agricultural advisory services | 2.2 Build a network of agricultural extension officers to support farmers in the use of climate information for improved decision making |
| 2.1 Local level climate change adaptation strategy | 2.3 Develop local level climate change adaptation strategies and policies |
| 2.2 Climate change adaptation training | 2.4 Strengthen damage assessment capacity at the local level |
| 2.3 Increase access to climate information services | 2.5 Improve access to climate information for vulnerable groups |
| 2.4 Build a network of agricultural extension officers to support farmers in the use of climate information for improved decision making |

### Theory of Change
The project aims to increase the productivity, profitability, and sustainability of smallholder agriculture through more stable and increased yields, leading to improved adaptive capacity of the most vulnerable people.

## Drivers/barriers

### Increasing temperatures
- Promotion of Climate-smart Agriculture (CSA) (output 2:2)
- Improved timing of planting, agronomy, crop variety and rotation (output 2:2)
- Improved post-harvest storage (output 3:1)
- Use of shade mutual shading material (e.g. straw bales, shelters), weeding, etc.

### Increased precipitation variability
- Promotion of CSA (output 2:2)
- Improved timing of planting, agronomy, crop variety and rotation (output 2:2)
- Integrated Pest management (IPM) (output 3:1)
- Pastoral system, water harvesting and storage

### Increase in frequency and intensity of droughts
- Promotion of CSA (output 2:2)
- Improved timing of planting, agronomy, crop variety and rotation (output 2:2)
- Shift to drought-tolerant crops, improved soil moisture retention (output 3:1)

### Lack of planning and weak local governance
- Improved legal frameworks and institutional capacity of ministries (output 3:1)
- Improved legal frameworks and institutional capacity of lead farmers (output 2:2)
- WUAC (output 3:1)

### Insufficient dissemination of technology
- Climate informed agricultural advisory services (output 3:1)
- Improved legal frameworks and institutional capacity of ministries (output 3:1)
- Improved legal frameworks and institutional capacity of lead farmers (output 2:2)
- WUAC (output 3:1)
- Deployment of early warning climate information services, Agriculture advisory services (outputs 3:2)

### Agricultural & livestock
- Promotion of Climate-smart Agriculture (CSA) (output 2:2)

## B

### Low and equal access to productive assets

62 The second type of beneficiaries include cooperatives (particularly seed growers), community-based associations (for example water user association committees), micro and small agricultural enterprises (particularly processors, transporters, traders, and other service providers), and women’s groups. These groups are particularly vulnerable as they receive training and capacity building from the project.

### The project will support a wide range of cooperatives and associations, including for example FFS or APFS, cooperatives, producer associations, savings groups, and small agro-businesses. In addition, the project also provides technical assistance to the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change (MECC) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MoAI) and its build their capacity to provide support services for these cooperatives.

### The project, through the Gender Action Plan, will seek to ensure the fullest possible participation of women as decision-makers and agents of change, recognizing the limitations and barriers they currently face in accessing land, productive resources, credit, markets, and other services. The project will also take steps to minimize any risk of violence or harassment that could be exacerbated by their increased access to assets and influence.

#### Project description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

66 The project is structured along three main outcomes. The three outcomes work together to create a context in which, on the one hand, communities participate in the restoration of the productive landscape; and, on the other hand, they are able to access the resources needed for sustainable livelihoods. These three inter-related and mutually reinforcing outcomes support the transition from unsustainable, unproductive, and mismanaged production systems to more resilient landscapes that can provide a basis for improved livelihoods. The project builds upon lessons learned from recent projects and programs and are closely coordinated with other efforts to rebuild the country following years of conflict.

#### For each activity, site, and beneficiary eligibility criteria are specified in Annex 2, FIS sections 8.4 and 8.5. The specific activities, sites, and beneficiaries will be determined during project implementation as set out in Annex 1.

Outcome 1—Restored landscapes are resilient and sustainably managed.

### Output 1 Improved participatory landscape and natural resources management and governance are established at watershed and village levels.

#### Activity 1.1 Strengthen the information base for climate-informed local land use planning

**1.1.1 Land cover and land degradation assessment**

- **Land Cover**: The project will build on the existing national land cover map, which was improved and more adapted vegetation approaches that are based on scientific data, participatory and democratic processes, and climate risk analysis.
- **Level of Land Degradation**: A country-level baseline study at a lower scale is necessary to be able to determine the area's most risk and to develop appropriate mitigation measures. The project will mobilize the expertise of the data networks that have been developed for the SIRALIM project, and mix satellite imagery with ground-based observation to characterize the following key parameters:
  - Land Cover
  - Level of Land Degradation

**1.1.2 Develop climate-informed inclusive landscape management plans (LMPs)**

- Based on the data provided above the project will strengthen the capacity of local actors to conduct participatory landscape assessments and develop climate-informed land use planning.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Landscape Management Planning Process

The landscape management planning process will bring together actors representative of various groups from across the sub-watershed. Each group or sub-watershed will nominate a representative, for a total of 30 representatives in the landscape management committee (LMC). The LMC will be responsible for overseeing the project (where possible).

The project will facilitate the nomination process in its first year. Members of the LMC will receive training on climate change adaptation and mitigation, land use planning, participatory planning, gender equality, social safeguards, and on committee governance, rules, and by-laws (sub-activity 1.1.2.3). Each LMC will then undertake a landscape assessment and develop a landscape management plan.

### Landscape Management Plans

Landscape Management Plans aligned to sub-watersheds (31 in total) will be established for a period of 6 years and will include the following:

- Assessment of land and water resources and their degradation using data collected in activity 1.1.1, presented in a user-friendly format
- Mapping of land use types, uses, and an overview of land use rights
- Land use plans and allocations by use (e.g., strategic green infrastructure for water catchment, conservation, etc.)
- Selection and characterization of priority landscape restoration and EAS activities to be delivered over the project period
- Budget for design and the maintenance/operations of landscape management

The activities under output 1.4 are delivered through FAD or EE. By the end of the second year of the project, it is expected that 33 LMPS will be validated with a set of priority interventions approved by each committee.

### Output 1.2 Agricultural and Agropastoral Landscapes are restored and sustainably managed

#### Activity 1.2.1 Conduct landscape restoration through local landscape management committees and community-based associations

The project supports two types of ecosystem-based restoration activities according to the specific locations: one, which is designed to enhance the water-related ecosystem functions (run-off control, erosion control, soil moisture conservation) in areas where land degradation is mainly due to high temperatures, run-off, and evaporation; and another, soil organic matter, erosion control, and control of invasive species. The options for landscape restoration include terraces, contour bunds, surface dams, riverbanks, and hafirs/water pans. These activities are described in Annex 2 of Prospekt. This project will procure required material and provide training to LMC members on the functions and expected results of each type of activity (sub-activity 1.2.1.1), in order to facilitate supervision. Details on each restoration option can be found in Annex 2, Chapter 6.

| Restoration Option | Climate problem | Landscape degradation issue |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hot Moisture Deficient Areas | Aridity, high temperatures, run-off and evaporation, land degradation | Land degradation, reduction in soil organic matter. Low productivity. |
| Contour bunds | Aridity, high temperatures, run-off, land degradation | Land degradation, erosion of arable land. |
| Surface dams | Aridity, high temperatures, run-off, land degradation | Run-off, soil erosion, flooding |
| Riverbanks | Aridity, high temperatures, run-off and evaporation, land degradation | Run-off, soil erosion, flooding |
| Hafirs/Water Pans | Aridity, run-off, land degradation | Land degradation, erosion of arable land. |
| Soil Organic Matter Control of Prospekt | Aridity, land degradation, soil erosion, loss of arable land. | Low productivity. |

B

78. The project will also rehabilitate eroded riverbanks in targeted areas in the South-West and Hirschelbahn regions (sub-activity 1.2.1). The activities take place on communal land or public land and are designed to provide resilient livelihoods from agriculture, livestock production, and fisheries. The rehabilitation of riverbanks will be complemented by on-farm land and water management activities that will be implemented under Outcome 2. For technical assistance, the project will engage with local communities.

79. Landscape management committees (LMCs) will receive trainings on monitoring on land restoration activities (sub-activity 1.2.4). The LMCs will also be trained in hydro-meteorological monitoring and climate change adaptation strategies and on monitoring on these land restoration activities (sub-activity 1.2.4). Sites will be equipped with hydro-meteorological stations to monitor rainfall, temperature, humidity, wind speed, and other relevant parameters. A set of indicators that link Inland Farming Directly, linked directly with sub-activity 1.1.1 and sub-activity 2.1.1. Each LMC will develop a monitoring plan for the land restoration activities in their area. The hydro-meteorological stations will be installed through awareness workshops. The hydro-climate equipment will be managed by MWA during the project execution according to SWALIM’s guidelines. After completion of the project, SWALIM functions will be gradually transferred over to the government.

**Outcome 2 Local livelihoods are resilient to climate change:**

80. Activities under this outcome are designed to ensure that local communities derive increased and improved climate resilient livelihoods from agriculture. The project targets existing value chains (maize, sesame, sorghum) which are already adapted to climate variability. The project will also introduce new crops such as pigeon pea and cowpea. The project also supports the transition from extensive to intensive cropping and facilitates the transition from pastoralism to agro-pastoralism. The project will support the development of a market for agricultural products, particularly on securing resilient water supply for agricultural production through contributions of Ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures.

Output 2.1 Resilient water supply is secured and sustainably managed:

81. Similarly to the activities under Output 1, participatory governance of water resources will be established as a primary mechanism for ensuring that water resources are managed in a manner that is sustainable and climate sensitive. The project works with the government to establish data systems and governance mechanisms to ensure access to information on water resources and their use by communities and local communities to help them from access improved and resilient water supply.

**Art 2.1.1 Strengthening Water User Associations (WUAs) at Local and State level:**

82. In the perspective of decentralizing and democratizing natural resources management, the project will support the establishment and capacity of water user association committees (WUCs), that will oversee the rehabilitation, maintenance, and operation of water infrastructure in their areas (sub-activity 2.1.1). The WUCs will be established at the local level (sub-activity 2.1.1a) and at project sites, WUAs will be placed under the overall umbrella of landscape management committees to ensure consistency with the land restoration activities. Each WUC will have a clear mandate for water resource management, mobilization, allocation, and maintenance. Each WUC Board will be formed of approximately 50 people (sub-activity 2.1.1b). The WUCs will be established in a participatory manner based on social acceptability and on conviction that the communities are willing to contribute towards water infrastructure. The WUCs will receive leadership, governance, and management training, as well as training on climate-informed operations and maintenance of water infrastructure (sub-activity 2.1.1c). The project will also provide technical assistance in the material means for conducting supervision and monitoring of water infrastructure.

83. To ensure that WUCs are able to effectively manage and maintain long-term operationalization of water infrastructure, the project will partner with UN Capital Development Fund (UNCDF) support work with the WUCs to establish a revolving fund (sub-activity 2.1.1d). The revolving fund will be used for maintenance of water infrastructure is maintained using community contributions. This will require working with WUC and their constituencies to ensure that they are able to contribute towards the maintenance of water infrastructure (sub-activity 2.1.1e). Fees will be established in a participatory manner, based on social acceptability and on conviction that the communities are willing to pay for services they are using via the water infrastructure. The fees mechanism might be updated in other districts under other financing.

84. At State level, to support the devolution of water management, the project will support the delivery of a water accounting exercise (sub-activity 2.1.1f). This activity will involve training of MWA staff at State level on water accounting and the use of the Water Accounting System (WAS) developed by FAO in an open data system available made to MuKi, MoECC and all State actors for improved knowledge and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Activity 2.1.2 Increase access to water resources and climate-smart irrigation infrastructure

### Background
In order to secure resilient livelihoods for vulnerable communities, access to water for irrigation needs to be upgraded and enhanced. The project area is characterized by a high level of vulnerability due to the following factors: (i) most areas have a dearth of water mobilization and conservation infrastructures; and those in place have either been destroyed during the 2015-2016 drought or are not functioning properly, (ii) communities have made significant efforts to stabilize population and ensure their continued food security as a success, water availability must be ensured for irrigation purposes. The project will address these issues by rehabilitating existing infrastructure in the project area, as well as the data from the soil and water baseline study under output 1.0, the project will upgrade and rehabilitate all existing irrigation infrastructure (canals, ditches, and other water conveyance systems) within the Sabun Barrage and the primary canal that provides water to 4 districts (Lowale, Agowe, Mekra and Batokai), serving a total of approximately 105,000 people. The project will also rehabilitate existing irrigation infrastructure executed by FAO). The project will upgrade, rehabilitate, and operationalize 16 irrigation canals (will in canals representing 24 km of canal length) and 3 ditches (representing a total of approximately 5 km of ditch length).

### Outputs
All rehabilitated infrastructures along with supplied irrigation equipment (drip irrigation and solar pumping systems) will be managed and operated by WUAC established in activity 1.0, according to agreed water uses and rules for both the rehabilitation and new infrastructure. The project will also provide training on drip irrigation techniques and producers, including drip irrigation kits and solar-operated pumping systems (sub-activity 2.1.3.2). This will enable farmers to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices.

### Details of Infrastructure to be Rehabilitated
Details of the infrastructure to be rehabilitated can be found in Annex 2, chapter 6.2.

## Output 2.2 Local communities practice locally specific Climate Resilient Agriculture

### Activities under this output are designed to transfer climate resilient agriculture (CRA) technologies, approaches, and practices that will be promoted through this project to local extension services for the support provided by local extension services to the adoption of climate resilient agricultural practices.

### Activity 2.2.1 Establish CRA technology demonstration sites
The project primarily adopts two from the Farmer Field School (FFS) and Agroforestry Field School (AFS) models, which have been successfully used globally by FAO to rapidly disseminate better food production practices. The "climate-resilient" FFS model is based on a participatory approach that involves farmers in identifying climate systems and value chains, priorities of the local community. Selection of specific technologies and practices will be guided by the results from the baseline study under output 1.0, as well as the outputs of FFS and FSS in the region. For example, FFS members may be invited to participate in LMC meetings, some may also be utilized for demonstration purposes. The project will establish a total of 25 CRA technology demonstration sites. Table B.3.2 lists all climate adaptation technologies and their benefits.

| Technology | Adaptation benefit | Climate Hazards, Decrease of vulnerability and Impact reduced |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Biogas | Production of biogas helps reduce pressure on forests and woodlands, which are important sources of firewood and fuel. | Land degradation; Deforestation |
| Cover crops | Maintenance of soil fertility, reduction of erosion due to wind and rain; increased yields and access to food. | Soil erosion (wind and rainfall), soil compaction, saltlogging, flood, erratic rainfall patterns |
| Crop rotation | Improved nutrient cycling, increased access to food and nutrition. | Reduced pests, floods, erratic rainfall patterns |
| Drip irrigation (Solar) | Increased water use efficiency and productivity for key crops. Improved water use efficiency. | Increased temperature; drought and extended dry seasons; reduced soil erosion |

- Intercropping: Increased land productivity, diversification, improved soil fertility and soil moisture.

- Reduced land degradation, reduced risk of erosion, increased soil fertility, improved productivity.

- Reduced land degradation, reduced risk of erosion, increased soil fertility, improved productivity.

- Reduced soil erosion, reduced risk of erosion, increased soil fertility, improved productivity.

## Use of nitrogen-fixing plants
- Increased soil fertility, improved productivity.

- Increased soil fertility, improved productivity.

- Increased productivity of crop and livestock under climate change.
- Improved water retention: Increased access to irrigation in farm and household level (household-level water storage).

- Increased water retention, increased access to water for livestock (e.g. through watering gardens), leading to increased production.

- Increased soil fertility, reduced risk of land degradation and risk of erosion.
- Improved livestock production: Reduced risk of erosion, improved soil moisture and water conservation.

## Contour farming
- Greenhouses, net houses or protected cropping: Reduced crop losses from extreme heat, improved productivity and efficiency.

- Increased livestock production and diversification, improved access to nutritious food, increased income.
- Tree-based systems for fodder, income, and synoecological systems for fodder.

- Heatwaves and other extreme events

90. The project will organize 420 FFS and APFS in total, grouping 30 people each (man and women). At the beginning of the project, FAO with MoA will develop a curriculum that will include detailed technical packets on all the proposed technologies. These will be distributed to the facilitators who will train farmers, some of whom may be selected from among existing extension officers in project districts, will receive extension training at the national level and will be provided with practical experience through the incorporation of the technologies in Somalia (sub-activity 2.2.1.2).

91. FAO will provide technical and technical support in addition to regular field visits and knowledge visits over the course of two years (sub-activities 2.2.1.3). The Government of Somalia, through MoA and its network of extension officers will provide on-the-job training and logistical backing, supervision and monitoring of the FTS throughout the project (sub-activity 2.2.1.4).

### Activity 2.2.2 Build the capacity of MoA at Local, State and Federal level to support communities in the adoption of climate resilient practices

92. In parallel, the project will strengthen the technical capacity of MoA at local, State and Federal level to support provincial extension officers in their role as facilitators for FTS. The lack of adequate training and the low level of investment into extension, agricultural research and agricultural information services, has undermined the capacity of extension officers to provide farmers with the necessary knowledge and skills required to implement a service-receiving program related to climate resilient agriculture (sub-activity 2.2.2.1), which will be developed based on the needs assessment conducted in sub-activity 2.2.1.1. The training will be provided by FAO at the national level of the project and will also be deployed to all MoA recruits at federal and State levels, through NGOs and with the collaboration of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MoAL). The training will be conducted in close cooperation with MoA as part of its regular training mandates. Short-term training for technical staff and extension staff who are involved in the project will also be provided by FAO at the national level, through NGOs and with the collaboration of MoA as part of its regular training mandates. The main objective of these trainings will be to build capacity among extension officers to provide farmers with knowledge and dissemination of the climate resilient agricultural practices promoted by the project (sub-activity 2.2.2.2).

### Output 2.3: Communities increase income from sustainable natural resource management and climate resilient values.

93. Under output 2.3, activities are targeted towards the development of value chains that are more resilient. While output 2.1 is aimed at increasing agricultural productivity through the adoption of climate-resilient practices, output 2.3 is aimed at actors beyond producing households. The purpose of these activities will be to ensure that the benefits from increased agricultural productivity are fairly distributed and sufficiently organized and structured to ensure continuity and gradual upscaling of project outputs and activities.

### Activity 2.3.1 Strengthen the seed distribution system

94. This activity is aimed at strengthening and reorganizing the seed distribution system, in line with MoA priorities and with the needs of local producers. There is an urgent need to secure a stable supply of resilient seed material, to ensure that farmers have access to quality seeds for their crops. The project will support the development of a resilient seed value chain by strengthening the seed distribution system through the establishment of a national seed agency. This includes fodder and crop varieties. As a first element, the project will support a comprehensive seed market dynamics analysis, which will include an assessment of the current seed market structure, an analysis of demand-supply projections aligned to climate scenarios, and an analysis of prices and storage capacities. This study will be conducted by FAO in collaboration with MoA and other stakeholders.

95. The MoA will also receive extensive technical support and training on the quality assurance, certification and registration of seeds (sub-activity 2.3.1.1). The National Seed Agency (NSA) will be established to ensure that seed certification is carried out in a timely manner through the establishment of improved test and trial sites and laboratory testing facilities (sub-activity 2.3.1.2).

96. The project will aim to strengthen the seed supply system in multiple ways: first, by acquiring appropriate, internationally recognized quality seeds from international suppliers; secondly, by establishing a national seed agency for the purpose of seed certification and registration; thirdly, by supporting farmers’ associations or cooperatives who will act as seed mutiples (sub-activity 2.3.1.4). These seed multipliers will be trained in seed multiplication techniques to ensure that they are capable of becoming autonomous seed providers by the end of the project. During the project, the project will acquire seeds from international suppliers and will establish a national seed agency for the purpose of seed certification (sub-activity 2.3.1.5). By the end of the project, pricing for the seeds will be established together with the cooperatives and

## Activity 2.3.2 Build the capacity of producer groups to develop sustainable climate-informed business plans.

105. In order to further strengthen the value chains, the producer groups, cooperatives and associations will also be strengthened in their ability to manage risk and adapt to climate change through the development of a business plan for each group. A key element of ensuring resilient livelihoods can be pursued throughout the region. The project will support 110 groups (sub-activity 2.3.2.1), cooperatives (sub-activity 2.3.2.2) and associations (sub-activity 2.3.2.3). These groups will focus on the production of sesame, sorghum, millet, maize, groundnuts, rice, beans, cowpeas, soybeans, cotton, peanuts, sesame, nutmegs, produce, cocoa, coffee, tea and vanilla (sub-activity 2.3.2.1). The seed groups will also receive training on value addition, processing, reduced post-harvest losses and marketing of their products (sub-activity 2.3.2.1). When necessary, the project may provide input supply services to these producer groups (see sub-activity 2.3.2.4). Where necessary, the project may also provide inputs for small producers and/or such as small processing equipment equivalent to a mini mill (sub-activity 2.3.2.5). The project will support the construction of climate resilient food storage facilities including seed production units, cold rooms, drying facilities, etc., which will facilitate access to market, price control, and import/export, reduce wastes and losses for value added commodities.

## Activity 2.3.3 Increase MSME, cooperatives and farming group access to agricultural finance

106. In order to further strengthen the value chains, the project will provide technical support in accessing agricultural credit and rural finance, also as a way to strengthen the sustainability of value chains, and to ensure better market access for smallholder farmers. The project will support 250 groups (sub-activity 2.3.3.1), cooperatives (sub-activity 2.3.3.2) and associations (sub-activity 2.3.3.3). A subset of the DFS and ARPS groups will be supported in establishing a savings group, which will provide them with access to financial services such as savings accounts, microfinance, governance, financial literacy and bookkeeping, and loan making (sub-activity 2.3.3.1). The purpose of VSLA will be for the groups to save money and access credit from their own savings pool. This will also include training on climate resilience requirements. As part of sub-activity 2.3.3.1, the project will also use the Gender Action Learning System (GALS) approach to ensure that women are included in all aspects of the project activities including households groups. These groups, cooperatives and small businesses (including seed mills, fodder producers and nurseries) will be supported by financial institutions that will also be capacitated to support climate resilience investments (sub-activity 2.3.3.2).

108. One promising model, which has been tested in Somalia and provides unique advantages in terms of commercialization and market access for smallholder farmers is the Community Based Agribusiness Model resembling contract farming and secures income for vulnerable producer households whose production is guaranteed by a buyer. The project will support 50 groups (sub-activity 2.3.3.1) to establish such contracts with private sector buyers. The project will develop 4% between farmer cooperatives, microfinance institutions, Somali Bankaries Association and local agricultural enterprises. This will be done through the establishment of a revolving fund.

## Activity 2.3.4 Working on the supply side of the rural finance continuum

109. The project will also provide technical support to the government in developing a rural finance strategy that will address the needs of smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups in response to the needs of the sesame, sorghum and maize value chains in a context of climate change and climate resilience. This strategy will include access to credit, insurance, savings accounts, equity, insurance, lines of credit or other forms of accessible finance for small producers and small groups.

108. Additional details on key stakeholders, existing institutions and potential partners are found in Annex 2: Chapter 6.2.

Activity 2.3.4 Involves all-season access to market for smallholder producers, cooperatives and farmer groups

105. Finally, the project will invest in rehabilitating rural infrastructure that has been degraded either due to conflict or natural causes in order to ensure that producers can access markets to both acquire inputs and sell produce. This rehabilitation of rural infrastructure is a key component of the project’s strategy for increasing market access, offer important coping strategies on multiple levels. From an economic perspective, access to markets allows the creation of new income opportunities for smallholder farmers and other rural households. In addition, by creating context functioning and accessible markets create poles where households can access social services and safety nets, as well as health care facilities. The rehabilitation of rural infrastructure will also contribute to climate resilience: the project will rehabilitate rural roads and upgrade them to withstand harsher climate conditions, including heat and severe rainfall events. This will allow for better transport of agricultural products from farms to markets. In addition, the project will also rehabilitate cattle corridors and create intermediary markets at district level or regrouping points at sub-district level. The rehabilitation of rural infrastructure is a key component of the project’s strategy for increasing market access, offer important coping strategies on multiple levels. In addition, by creating context functioning and accessible markets create poles where households can access social services and safety nets, as well as health care facilities. The rehabilitation of rural infrastructure will also contribute to climate resilience: the project will rehabilitate rural roads and upgrade them to withstand harsher climate conditions, including heat and severe rainfall events. This will allow for better transport of agricultural products from farms to markets. In addition, the project will also rehabilitate cattle corridors and create intermediary markets at district level or regrouping points at sub-district level.

Outcome 2.3.5: Improved institutional environment for sustainable landscape management and climate resilient agriculture is in place at State and Federal levels

106. Activities under Outcome 3 form part of the project’s sustainability and operating strategy. The aim is to create conditions that will ensure long-term sustainability, including adoption of project results. Given the fragile conditions of the Southern Corridor of Somalia, strengthening of capacity is required to ensure that Federal, State, local governments and other actors have the necessary tools and mechanisms in place to implement the activities. The activities will include: developing norms and standards for landscape management; building institutional capacity (through frameworks and modalities for monitoring their implementation including performance indicators); building technical capacity to generate and disseminate useful, relevant and timely climate information to take into account.

Output 3.1 Legal frameworks and implementation modalities for NRM and CRA are improved

Activity 3.1.1 Update legal and regulatory frameworks for sustainable landscape management

107. The project will work with the Federal government to ensure that changes are to be enacted to the national legislative and policy apparatus to support the transition towards climate-resilient development pathways. This includes, in particular, the inclusion of landscape management as a key component of the National Land Use Policy (NLP) and other related by-laws and other instruments to consolidate landscape management and upscale it to the rest of the country. (Sub-activity 3.1.1.2). The project will also work with the Federal government to ensure that changes are enacted in the national manual, tailored to the specific Somali context, covering all the elements of the landscape approach on the basis of lessons learned from the pilot phase.

109. The project will also work with MoCCG to develop a strategy and action plan on the management of Prosopis, as an invasive species that severely restricts agricultural development potential. The Strategy and action plan will also build capacity for landscape management at all levels (national, state, district and sub-district) towards achieving outcome 1, and in the results reported by landscape management committees in terms of benefits of such actions. This includes: developing a strategy to address the problem of Prosopis; identifying areas where it is present or likely to spread; defining the appropriate measures for its control; preparing plans for construction, buildings and infrastructure that will eliminate the principle of resilience and will formalize adoption or any other relevant policy instruments; and ensuring that all landscape management committees are aware of the assessments conducted in the project (sub-activity 3.1.1.5). Standards on climate proofing infrastructure will be defined to ensure that it is resilient, sustainable and does not compromise the environment and does not restrict extreme weather events such as floods.

Outcome 2.3.6: Improved intersectoral coordination between sectoral ministries at State levels

110. Furthermore, the project will work at national and State level to increase intersectoral coordination between various ministries and vertical coordination from the local to the federal level. The landscape management approach projects that all relevant actors are involved in the planning process and that they have a clear understanding of adaptation benefits for vulnerable communities. The project therefore will assess the existing coordination
