# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 25

## Activity 3.1.3 Strengthen the capacity of MEECC to manage, monitor and govern natural resources and implement Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA)

### 1.11 To support effective decentralization and to create an enabling environment in which landscape management is implemented at all levels, the project will provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MEECC) to plan and monitor EBA solutions to climate change. Training will be provided to district, State and Federal level MEECC staff on EBA solutions to climate change. The training will include a mix of theoretical and practical components focusing on staff that will be acting as support to landscape management committees established under output 1.1.1 (sub-activity 3.1.2). Training will also be provided to the landscape management committees themselves (sub-activity 3.1.2). This will be particularly important to ensure that all stakeholders are aware and can respond effectively to climate change impacts on natural resources, including water availability and quality. The training will serve to facilitate broader upscaling and replication later on. To this end, the MEECC at the FM level will be supported in mainstreaming EBA solutions into its work plan and budget (sub-activity 3.1.2). This will include the development of a national action plan for EBA as well as field-testing facilities to support continued research into the application of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) at the FM level. The MEECC will also be supported in mainstreaming EBA solutions into its work plans and budget, as well as supporting the implementation of the Environmental Management and Protection Act (sub-activity 3.1.2) and the operationalization of the Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (sub-activity 3.1.2).

### Activity 3.1.4 Build capacity for the monitoring, assessment analysis and early warning related to the impacts of climate on food security

#### 1.12 Similarly, the MoU, local universities and NGOs will also receive support to conduct monitoring, assessment and early warning of food security at decentralized levels (sub-activity 3.1.4.1). This will include provision of training and technical assistance for district level staff in the areas of climate change adaptation and mitigation as well as climate conditions. In order to develop more reliable food security warnings at district level. Each district will then conduct regular monitoring, assessment analysis and early warning related to the impacts of climate on food security (sub-activity 3.1.4.2). This will be linked to the existing IPCC working group and national focal points for climate change adaptation and mitigation as well as the National Climate Change Secretariat. The information generated by this activity will also be disseminated through local media, print and radio (sub-activity 3.1.4.3).

### Activity 3.1.5 Build capacity of MEECC for climate informed water management infrastructure planning

#### 1.14 The project will support technical assistance to the ministry of agriculture and irrigation to develop climate information products that can be used in the development of water management plans at all levels, from the national level down to the river basin. The process will be participatory and will include the development of a multi-stakeholder task force, as well as the development of a climate-informed water management plan (sub-activity 3.1.5.1). This will include the development of an irrigation task force plan (sub-activity 3.1.5.1) which will coordinate and conduct consultations with water and land users to develop a multi-stakeholder task force for the development of climate-informed water management plans at all levels, as well as the development of a climate-informed master plan sub activity (sub-activity 3.1.5.2). The objective of having a national level task force is to ensure that the development of plans for irrigation water supply and flood protection in order to prepare other initiatives and prioritize future investment.

### Output 3.2 Increased Access to Climate Information Among Last Mile Users

#### 1.17 At present, many communities at last mile level do not have sufficient access to climate information services. Forecasts are not yet being shared from national to State or local level, and their reliability is not necessarily high enough for decision making purposes. Furthermore, the current system of dissemination of forecasts for climate extremes are not being disseminated, leading to misinformed early adaptation action at local levels. The development of a multi-stakeholder task force that will be responsible for the last mile climate service delivery in order to ensure that the transition to climate-resilient livelihoods will be incomplete and unsustainable.


---
**Page 31**
---

# Activity 3.2.1: To disseminate and share relevant climate and land data to support decision making at all levels

The project will work with the MoCCD and the climate information service providers at Federal and State levels to redesign and deploy an open access GIS-based climate information platform, on which users will be able to access and use climate and land data. The climate information service providers will also be linked to agronomic advice (sub-activity 3.2.1). All information will be made publicly available free of charge through the platform. The project will ensure that the climate information service providers and meteorological and agro-meteorological staff will also receive training and capacity building for the deployment and use of the system.

# Activity 3.2.1: To disseminate and share relevant climate and land data to support decision making at all levels

To ensure that last mile users receive information and can act on it in a timely and appropriate manner, the project will work with the MoCCD to establish an Information Management Centre (IMC) at the State level for early warning and decision support tools for agricultural producers (sub-activity 3.2.1). The activity will build on the lessons learned from the pilot phase of the project in which IMCs were established at the Federal, State or through the relay group leaders (FFS, associations, savings groups, cooperatives). Information will be free of charge and available to all users. The IMC will be a focal point for climate information services provided by the system, including exploring cost-sharing mechanisms, cost recovery and pricing options for long-term affordability of the service on the part of the end-users (sub-activity 3.2.1).

# Activity 3.2.1: To disseminate and share relevant climate and land data to support decision making at all levels

In order to further enhance the capacity of the MoCCD to undertake climate informed landscape management and planning, the project will work with the MoCCD to establish 2 SWALIM information management centres (IMCs) at decentralised level. The IMCs will be responsible for collecting, processing, analysing, storing and sharing on all aspects related to natural resources. The centres will be tasked with providing climate information services to end-users through the relay group leaders (FFS, associations, savings groups, cooperatives). The IMC will be developed as part of this project (under outcome 1) and distributing information that can serve to display climate-informed landscape management and planning at decentralised level. The IMCs will be established and managed by MoCCD staff and operated by MoCCD and MoMh, as part of the institutionalization of SWALIM, and thereby ensuring sustainability of the project (sub-activity 3.2.1). The IMC will be modelled on the existing SWALIM IMCs that has successfully been established in the pilot phase.

# Activity 3.2.1: To disseminate and share relevant climate and land data to support decision making at all levels

Finally, the project will support the development of radio programs, awareness and distance learning targeting the population (sub-activity 3.2.1). Although there is an ongoing transition in the Somali media landscape as on-line platforms are increasingly becoming more popular than traditional media such as radio or television, radio or the radio online service is quickly spread throughout the community. The awareness raising radio programs will be developed and disseminated through local radio stations reaching the communities at the district and community level.

# B. Implementation arrangements (max 1500 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

## Accredited Entity

The FAF will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project. As such, FAF will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project approval; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight of the project implementation; (iii) ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with approved results and achieves objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to GCF; (v) ensuring that the project complies with all applicable laws, regulations and policies; and (vi) complying with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAF and GCF.

## Project Co-financing

FAF will be fully funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The FAF will be composed of the Budget Holder (BH), the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Liaison Officer (FLO), HQ Technical Officer (HQ TO).

The PTF will remain independent from the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAF (see Project execution arrangements). The PTF will be led by the Lead Technical Officer (LTO) and composed of the above-mentioned segregation of responsibilities within FAF will ensure that the Organization can independently and effectively manage the project. The PTF will be funded through GCF projects and is not subject to eligible costs criteria under GCF fees and project management costs.


---
**Page 32**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 28

## Executing Entities

The project will be executed by FAO and the Government of Somalia acting through (i) the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change (MoECC), (ii) the Ministry of Health (MoH), and (iii) the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Irrigation and Cooperatives (MoALIC). FAO will act as EEU and will ensure strong country-driven delivery of project activities and will be in charge of the overall implementation. The MoECC, MoH, and MoALIC will be responsible for the execution of activities to ensure Somalia to benefit from the technical and operations experience of a specialized development assistance agency (FAO) and the capacity building of national counterparts. The MoECC, MoH, and MoALIC will provide technical assistance and advice on the design and implementation of activities under the three components. FAO will also exercise overall responsibility for the project implementation.

### Flow of funds and legal arrangements

In line with the project implementation arrangements outlined above, GCF funds will be disbursed to the AE through a Project Agreement (including co-financing terms) signed by the Government of Somalia, MoECC, MoH, and MoALIC. The Project Agreement will include the financial terms for the disbursement of GCF funds and MoECC, MoH, and MoALIC contributions. The project beneficiaries are the communities in the target areas.

## B

### Project Implementation Committee (PIC) Justification for GCF Funding request (max. 1000 words; approximately 2 pages)

#### Figure B.4.2 Investment arrangements

**B.x Justification for GCF funding request:**

- **Somalia:** One of the world's most fragile states and a DACS priority, Somalia is a country where the convergence of several challenges demands urgent attention.
- The nation grapples with a myriad of issues, including the agriculture sector already suffering from climate change impacts. The population growth rate is among the highest in Africa, while rapidly growing populations face extreme vulnerability to climate variability and climate change.
- In contrast, there are areas where scarcity is already an issue, such as water availability and food insecurity. According to the latest Integrated Phasing Classification (IPC), approximately 1.7 million children below the age of five were acutely malnourished in Somalia at the end of May 2018.

- In contrast, there are areas where scarcity is already an issue, such as water availability and food insecurity. According to the latest Integrated Phasing Classification (IPC), approximately 1.7 million children below the age of five were acutely malnourished in Somalia at the end of May 2018.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 32

The same efforts contributing to build the resilience of ecosystems and communities. Large-scale programs, such as those funded by the GCF are also relevant in the context of Somalia where emergency response is being conducted through a multi-sectoral approach that includes food security, shelter building, and disarmament, with urgent food aid and food security interventions taking the forefront. If recovery is to be sustainable, however, it must be integrated into long-term development planning. This will only be possible if climate change adaptation is mainstreamed in all sectors of the economy. The project will be conducted in a climate-informed manner. The country has just ended the HIPC transition program and has finally paid off its debt. As such, the government has been able to free up resources for development purposes while public expenditures are rising (1 billion USD in 2024, as a percent increase from 2023), over two thirds of resources originate from domestic sources.

## Somalia's ability to invest in crucial sectors such as water and agriculture remains limited. With limited fiscal space, public investment in agriculture remains dominantly slow, hindering the development of resilient farming practices and agricultural productivity. The government has been working on a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) since 2016, enabling the implementation of sustainable initiatives aimed at mitigating climate risks and improving productivity. This project will contribute to the NAP by providing technical assistance for the implementation of the NAP in which countries with adequate support can begin to create their own economic opportunities. Currently, Somalia is transitioning from a disaster response pathway to a development pathway, with a focus on building resilience through transitioning to climate-resilient development pathways, setting innovation, and hindering adaptation to climate change.

The government has expressed clear commitment to actively participate in international climate governance mechanisms and has been working towards the implementation of its NDC. The country's first NDC was submitted in 2016; and submitting its first National Communication in 2018 and the first Biennial Update Report in 2020. Since then, the government has been working on a second NDC which is currently under review. Work has been in process. Somalia's NDC 2021 estimated the cost of implementing adaptation actions of the NDC at $5 billion. The country has also submitted its first Biennial Update Report (BUR) to the UNFCCC, with an October 10 billion USD estimate as investment required for 2021-2030. As stated in Somalia's NDC, Somalia, as an LDC in a fragile state, is committed to implementing the NDC and has been working on the implementation of the NDC. These initiatives demonstrate a strong commitment on the country level to work with partners to implement climate change adaptation measures that will contribute to building resilience in the country.

The proposed GCF grant is appropriate to address the urgent needs of the country to cope with climate changes. Decades of conflict have taken a toll on Somalia's agricultural sector, exacerbating the vulnerability of local communities and undermining food security. The lack of infrastructure has also contributed to this problem, undermining agricultural productivity and exacerbating food insecurity. The degradation of natural and built infrastructure is a major obstacle in Somalia's efforts towards sustainable development and national development. These investments, however, lie beyond the means of the Somali government for the moment. In light of these challenges, the proposed project will provide technical assistance to the government as a crucial solution to sustain livelihoods, enhance food security, and foster socio-economic stability and resilience.

### Co-financing with other initiatives has been identified including the USAID-funded project (USD 15.1 million) to be implemented in the same region. The proposed project is complementary to existing projects, complementarity with other initiatives and sustainability.

#### Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will be phased over a period of three years with specific interventions. All activities are designed to be sustainable in the long term, and plans are integrated to ensure that the Somali government can maintain and upscale the key infrastructure and systems developed under this project.

#### First: The project creates a basis of information and knowledge that will be integrated into public policy making; for example, the baseline study on land cover and land degradation will be integrated into the information system created in phases 1, 2, 3, and 3.2 and will provide data to inform decision-making at all levels of government. Capacity built under this project will address climate conditions, crop adaptation and food security will also feed into long-term agricultural policy making and operations at both State and Federal levels. The project also includes

## Measures to strengthen the capacity of the government in terms of monitoring the adherence to rules and regulations related to natural resources use and management.

### 142.
At local level, the creation of landscape management committees and strengthening of water user committees will be key elements for ensuring sustainable and efficient management of natural resources within the context of decentralization. These committees will create lasting capacity in the communities to manage their own resources in a climate-informed manner. These institutional structures are expected to outlast the project duration and serve as a basis for long-term sustainability.

### 143.
Third, all infrastructure and landscape restoration activities are designed with an exit strategy in mind. The development of early warning systems, agrometeorological services, and other climate-resilient technologies will be complemented by capacity building to ensure their continued use after the project ends. In particular, all community groups (FIS, WUAC, LMC) will receive training and capacity building for the continued operation and maintenance of these systems.

### 146.
Care has been taken not to create systems or institutions that create undue financial burden on the beneficiaries or other Government. For example, under Outcome 3, work to develop early warning systems, agrometeorological services, and other climate-resilient technologies will be complemented by capacity building to ensure their continued use after the project ends.

### 147.
Another element of the project's exit and sustainability strategy is the strong emphasis on building the capacity of government actors in delivering support to local producing households. While it is understood that significant investments are required for the initial development of these systems, it is expected that most of this work will be owned and continued by the Government in the long term. This includes information management centres, early warning systems, agrometeorological services, and other climate-resilient technologies. The project will provide training programs that will fill both immediate capacity gaps and long-term gaps to ensure the climate risk management aspects are mainstreamed in all aspects of the MoCC's and MuKC's work.

### D.1 IMPACT POTENTIAL (MAX. 500 POINTS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

#### 131. ESTIMATED DIRECT BENEFICIARIES AND INDIRECT BENEFICIARIES

For output 1.1 since direct beneficiaries would be participants in LMC (960 people who are community members), they are included among other outputs. Indirect beneficiaries will be the entire population of the district, which is estimated at 253,742 people.

For output 1.2, direct beneficiaries represent all people served by areas under ecosystem-based adaptation, calculated according to the average size of per person (1 ha/person). Indirect beneficiaries are also the entire district population as above.

For output 2.1, the direct beneficiaries are the people served by areas under improved water management, irrigation and drainage systems, which is estimated at 30% of the total area in the district (85,932 hectares). The indirect beneficiaries will be the entire population of the district who will benefit from improved ecosystem services related to water. Direct beneficiaries include farmers and other land users who are directly involved in the project activities (e.g., 85,932 people). Direct beneficiaries of output 2.2 are cooperative members, producer group members who benefit from training and extension services.

Under Output 3.2, the direct beneficiaries are each person in district which can access climate information, estimated as a factor of the rate of access to mobile phones (44%). In both cases, the indirect beneficiaries represent the entire population of the district.

To avoid double counting, since the figure for output 3.2 also includes the others (as a sub-set), we have removed the direct beneficiaries from the total number of beneficiaries under Output 3.2 and added them to those who benefit from output 1.2 but are not counted under output 3.2. The Total indirect beneficiaries will be the entire district population as above.

The total number of beneficiaries is 405,867 (direct) + 253,742 (indirect) = 659,609 people. This includes all categories of beneficiaries, except in case of the activities that involve training government staff, where the proportion of women is lower than men.

#### 132. ADAPTATION BENEFITS

Adaptation benefits include increased yields of maize, sorghum, and sesame from the application of climate resilient agricultural practices and increased access to water (through better mastery of productive assets, both natural and man-made). The expected increase in yield is estimated at between 50-200% for each crop. For example, for maize, this can be significant, ranging broadly from 20-50% depending on the specific content and effectiveness of the intervention package (e.g., improved seed varieties, better irrigation systems, etc.). The expected increase in yield percentage increase in annual income per household (HH) after proposed interventions are implemented ranges from low to high. For example, for maize, this can be significant, ranging broadly from 20-50% depending on the specific content and effectiveness of the intervention package.

Assuming that the increase in income is at least partly earned by fees on food, these results should translate into increased household food security.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will address the drivers of land degradation in Somalia but will end through successive shocks and crisis in the last decades due to flanged drought increasing livestock as compared to the available forage resources. This has led to overgrazing, soil erosion, desertification, and loss of biodiversity in the target project district and alternative livelihoods.

The project will address the agricultural sector in Somalia from a common perspective of ongoing land degradation and crop and livestock losses and failure because of floods and drought, to one where yields are increased through improved water management practices, better use of fertilizers, and other inputs. This will lead to an increase in agricultural production, through infrastructure, rural finance, and value addition, so that households can exit their current poverty traps and enter a market-oriented economy that provides opportunities for diversification and builds social safety nets.

This project will enable producers to overcome barriers to market access by providing them with technology skills, marketing information, and access to credit. It will also provide technical assistance to the financial sector actors as well as to potential finance providers. A major change instituted in this project lies in the creation of mechanisms for the development of a sustainable agricultural economy through the establishment of pastoral production systems through local organizations such as landscape management committees and Water user associations.

The paradigm shift in this project lies in the integrated approach that targets not only producers, but also those who provide inputs (e.g., extension services providers, credit institutions, input suppliers, and financial institutions). The project will enable the existing externalized regional organizations at local, state, and federal levels to build their capacity for providing technical assistance. This will be achieved through training of trainers that builds national level systems and capacity to generate information that can be used by all States and state structures in Somalia. The knowledge generated from the various activities implemented under this project will also serve as a basis for the development of next generation of climate information products, which will be disseminated through mobile phones, radio, and media, and can also be updated rapidly.

All interventions are designed to be replicable at all levels and will be adapted at each level. The local level process set up for selecting and implementing landscape restoration and rehabilitation activities is a good example of this. At the state level, the project will build capacity in the various institutions that are implemented under the various activities will also serve as the development of next generation of climate information products, which will be disseminated through mobile phones, radio, and media, and can also be updated rapidly.

All interventions for agricultural production are based on best practice and can be adapted or upcycled to other regions. For example, the project will develop a model that can be used by extension services or through farmer networks themselves. In this regard the perception and communication of benefits from climate change adaptation measures is critical in order to ensure that the use of new sources of input markets will also serve as elements of this upscaling strategy, since they will facilitate the broader adoption of climate change adaptation practices.

### 160. Knowledge sharing and learning are essential components of this projects strategy. First the project will organize workshops and training sessions for all stakeholders involved in the project to ensure that they can inform policy and planning at local and government level. Second, the project will disseminate knowledge and best practices through a variety of channels including extension services, farmer field schools, and agricultural commodities. The mechanisms of transmission of information to end users include traditional extension services, FFS and APFs, community groups, awareness raising and use of mobile phones and media.

The project will ensure that all stakeholders involved in the project are equipped to interpret it and act upon it. This is why the project works closely with local communities and

**168.** The project also creates mechanisms for knowledge sharing among government partners. This includes projects that will be implemented between countries, cross-border planning and the mainstreaming of climate risks into development and territorial planning at all levels.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 170. **The environmental benefits** are:

- Ecosystem services: The project will implement land management plans through regeneration of the natural land cover using local resident species, through reforestation, rangeland rehabilitation and through the use of agroecological practices. This will lead to an increase in biodiversity, as well as a reduction in soil erosion and runoff from rainfall will be limited.
- Ecological services: The project will implement land management plans through regeneration of the natural land cover using local resident species, through reforestation, rangeland rehabilitation and through the use of agroecological practices. This will lead to an increase in biodiversity, as well as a reduction in soil erosion and runoff from rainfall will be limited.
- Indirectly, land cover is also expected to improve thanks to the introduction of terraces and/or off-contour structures listed above, once improved soil moisture is likely to help vegetation growth.

#### 171. The economic benefits the project will generate include: (i) income generation that will allow over-all improvement in the quality of life for rural communities; (ii) increased productivity through the use of integrated farming and livestock management practices; (iii) reduced costs associated with invasive species; (iv) additional income from value addition, food and input storage in community facilities; (v) improved access to markets for agricultural products; (vi) improved access to credit; (vii) links of the producers to microfinance institutions, to enable them to build their resilience to shocks and ensure that they can continue to produce.

#### 172. Main social benefits the project will generate include: (i) employment opportunities on farm participatory in improving agricultural practices through the use of agroecological practices; (ii) reduction in the spread of invasive species; (iii) promotion of youth, women and minorities engagement along the value chains, particularly in value addition activities; (iv) improved access to markets for agricultural products; (v) increased income for women; (vi) better management and prevention of local conflicts over natural resources management and strengthening of community-based institutions; (vii) reduced competition between agropastoralists and pastoralists in areas where the competition for grass and animal feeding is severe.

#### 173. Because of the objective to facilitate the transition from passive to active agro-pastoral systems and the capacity building required, the project will also contribute to the reduction of land degradation and resource management at local level.

### D.5. Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 500 words; approximate: 1 page)

The Project's expected impact, with an estimated total budget of USD 92 million, the project's GCF grant is estimated at USD 87.63 million. The project will benefit approximately 4.5 million people in Somalia, including beneficiaries, many agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists, and indirectly to 97.69 thousand people in Somaliland. This represents a coverage of about 10% of the total population of Somalia (which is estimated at 38.2 million) or approximately 45% of the population of Somalia's total population, which reached 17.0 million in 2023 (World Bank, 2022 ed.). The core project beneficiaries for GCF funding are agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists and USD 37.51 mil (or beneficiaries combined), or for total funding, USD 82.36 and USD 44.66 respectively.

Concessionally justified: The proposed concessional financing level for the Project is justifiable based on the following factors: poverty alleviation, food security, livelihoods, and climate change adaptation. The project addresses its development needs and contribute to achieving more sustainable economic growth. Various factors, including poverty, malnutrition, and internal government organization aspects, contribute to this need for grant financing:

The project will support the strengthening of national capacity in disaster risk management (DRM) linkages, and learning between the central and regional-level governing bodies. These activities will foster the integration of DRM into sectoral policies and plans, including agriculture, water resources, and health by customizing early warning systems (ii). Facilitating the provision and availability of public information to agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists on climate change adaptation practices and technologies. Knowing that these activities largely remain in the public good domain, per definition, they do not attract private sector financing.

The project will provide the necessary financial resources that could not have been obtained otherwise due to the lack of the necessary internal resources. Owing the IMF's press release on Somalia's budget and fiscal space five years from now, it is expected that the government of Somalia will be able to finance the project. It is appropriate to accommodate priority expenditures that are supportive of growth, security, and development while simultaneously ensuring that the public sector remains financially sustainable in order to ensure that the crucial to support the authorities' policy efforts.

The project will provide a safety net for agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists in a highly climate-vulnerable and poverty-stricken country to help them adapt to the onset of various weather and climate shocks: prolonged droughts, floods, and heatwaves. The project will also contribute to improving food security by enhancing food security and livelihood. The improved agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists are characterized by increased access to water resources, improved agricultural production systems, livestock production and animal herding to climate change-related weather patterns. They tend to have insufficient livestock numbers due to the lack of sufficient feed and water resources, which is a result of prolonged droughts due to the vulnerability of lending institutions (caused by farmers' remote location or non-sedentary livelihood), for example. The project will provide a safety net for agro pastoralists and marginalized pastoralists in Somalia: agropastoralists and marginalized pastoralists of goods that, in the long run, will contribute to the public good (i). This is achieved by providing them with access to improved agricultural production systems, livestock care, collection management, and water-saving techniques (iv). Management of encroaching plant invasive species.

Economic and financial analysis modeling: The indirect ex-ante EFA was pursued separately in financial and economic terms. The financial analysis model was used to assess the cost-effectiveness of financing the entire project with an embedded accounting of the budgetary implementation framework over 7 years. These two modeling perspectives (1 and 2) were additionally applied under two different climate exposure assumptions.


---
**Page 45**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 61

ROP 2.6 and RCP 8.5 to show how the results might change under differing climate handlings. The financial and economic EFA results were calculated over 20 years, using a conservative level of discount rate at 15%. The incremental financial NPV from the perspective of the entire project was estimated at USD 37.49 million (under RCP 2.6 assumption range from USD 6.25 million to USD 21.40 million). The incremental financial NPV from the perspective of the entire project was estimated at USD 58.47 million (under RCP 8.5 assumption range from USD 5.85 million to USD 31.90 million). The incremental aggregate Economic Net Present Values (ENPVs) of appraised six indicative CRAPs (i.e., indicative models) under RCP 2.6 assumption was estimated at USD 74.54 million, and the incremental ENPV from the perspective of the entire Project was estimated at USD 74.54 million. The incremental aggregate ENPVs of appraised six indicative CRAPs (i.e., indicative models) under RCP 8.5 assumption was estimated at USD 123.99 million, and the incremental ENPV from the perspective of the entire Project was estimated at USD 50.47 million. Therefore, the obtained results indicate that the expected benefits will outweigh the costs, and the proposed project should be implemented in its proposed form.

### Application of the best practices
The project aims to make the most impact by supporting the introduction of climate proof water infrastructure and promoting alternative livelihoods. Furthermore, the project will leverage the FAO’s Farm Field School (FFS) approach in project target districts. For instance, the project plans to leverage the FAO’s Farm Field School (FFS) approach in project target districts. Specifically, the project aims to rehabilitate and strengthen climate proof water infrastructure and promote alternative livelihoods through value chain development. The project will also build up interconnective capacities built-up interconnective the various elements to generate virtuous cycles of implementation, testing, and feedback. This will ensure that the project sustains efforts and generates financial and economic returns for intended beneficiaries and the entire economy

## Replicability

Currently there are a few examples of replicable approaches and practices which have been tested and proven but these have not been implemented at scale.

### Limit

Activities under output 2 are all replicable at the scale of the entire landscape (i.e., across all six regions of the country, representing a cross-section of different agro-ecological zones and land use zones. The participation of all six member states makes the replication of these activities realistically achievable. A national baseline study which is also plausible under certain conditions particularly the availability of funds and national stability.

### Activities that directly contribute to replication include:

1.1.1 The national baseline study which will help prioritize future interventions.
1.1.2 The Landscape Management Plans which will help scale up landscape level activities.
2.1.1 Which provides data on water accounting at State level to support future interventions and the creation of open-data systems.

## Sustainability

### Low

Sustainability of previous interventions has been compromised by the level of instability and conflict in the country.

As a recovering country, the creation of lasting institutions and programs will depend on the participation of all stakeholders. Conflict prevention and building measures are therefore appropriate responses to past interventions, with a view to strengthening the Federal government and members of society to enforce laws and provide valuable services to local populations.

### Approaches and strategies that will be used to ensure the sustainability of the project:

(i) participatory design and implementation of landscape management plans; (ii) establishment of community-based self-governance; (iii) knowledge management and sharing with stakeholders; (iv) creation of a monitoring system at local level that will ensure producers can access to credit and inputs, as well as the adoption of sustainable or eco-friendly practices; (v) capacity building for communities and project operators; vii) creation of a lasting data collection system to support decision making and climate informed decentralized governance.

## Activity 3.1 Strengthen the capacity of MoECC to manage, monitor and govern natural resources and experiment Ecosystem-based Adaptation

### Sub-activity 3.1.3 Training on Ecosystem-based Adaptation for MoECC
The MoECC will receive training and on-the-job support for the planning, management and monitoring of landscape restoration and EBA solutions. This will also include support on conducting participatory assessments of ecosystem services and adaptation benefits. The government will provide technical assistance and material assistance to conduct decentralized operations at State level.

### Sub-activity 3.1.4 Build capacity for monitoring, assessment, analysis and early warning systems in the context of climate-on food and nutrition security
The MoECC, local universities and NGOs will also receive support to conduct monitoring, assessment and early warning systems at decentralized levels. This will include training on the collection of data, assessment, analysis and early warning systems, production/availability and climate vulnerability assessments, and the development of reliable food security warnings as described above. Each State will establish a collaborative Food Security working group, seasonal assessment teams to monitor impacts of climate on food and nutrition.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

192 In its role as Accredited Entity (AE), FAO will oversee project implementation according to the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between TAD and AE. The AE will ensure that all project activities are implemented in accordance with the required standards. The M&E team will prepare draft performance reports, which will be sent to the PSFCPC. As per the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework, the AE will also provide a Final Evaluation Report (FER) to the PSFCPC within 12 months of project completion. The FER will be prepared by an independent Final Evaluation team. FAO will also provide semi-annual and annual Financial Reports throughout project implementation. The monitoring team will also share its findings with the CGI so that they may discuss implementation issues as they arise.


---
**Page 67**
---

193\. Project monitoring will be conducted by the M&E team composed of international and national M&E specialists. Information Management and Knowledge specialists will ensure the existence of a well-designed, operational, and effective impact monitoring and measurement dashboard based on project indicators. Project oversight will be carried out by the Project Technical and Steering Committees (PTC and PSC), the Central Project Implementation Unit (CPU), and the Budget Holder (BH). The M&E plan will be reviewed at least once per year to ensure that it remains relevant, and as necessary, will be adjusted as envisaged by the CPU, subject to the Budget Holder (BH) approval (FAO AE). Project components will be monitored separately as well as in relation to each other. Monitoring of project outputs and outcomes will be carried out through a combination of quantitative and qualitative methods using the data from the Monitoring Information System (MIS) to be developed by the CPU. The MIS will serve to track on the project implementation, including the outputs, outcomes, and impacts.

In compliance with the requirements in FAO’s Accreditation Master Agreement with the GCF FAD - Office of Evaluation (the Accredited Entity independent evaluation officers will conduct an independent evaluation of the project that meets GCF policy and Funded Activity Agreement obligations. To this end, the evaluation will cover multiple components aligned with GCF requirements such as assessing implementation and impacts. The evaluation budget includes all evaluation costs, including travel expenses (based on FAO rules) for field visits through surveys, focus groups, interviews, document analysis, report writing, and quality assurance. The Accredited Entity will communicate the results of this evaluation through an interim and a final independent evaluation report.

195\. Please refer to Annex 11 for more detail on the M&E plan.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Political instability: high rotation of government officers, potential changes in the development direction of the Government of Somalia and political landscape between IGAD and FMS

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Low enforcement of development priorities, strategies and commitments preventing from fully engaging in integrated landscape management and climate resilient agriculture. Turnover and changes in decision makers and institutional arrangements have led to a lack of continuity in the implementation of projects and programmes. The IGAD-FMS relationship (at least at Permanent Secretary level) and will debate together with the NSA about any changes in government policy and any other issues that may arise.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Project priorities were well aligned with the development priorities of Somalia and with the most recent national strategies such as NDC, NCD implementation plan. The project's implementation arrangements have foreseen that at least one permanent official from each institution will be involved in the project team. This arrangement is based on the Government commitment to anticipate potential changes in policies and strategic directives and to propose the necessary adjustments to the project team. The IGAD-FMS relationship (at least at Permanent Secretary level) and will debate together with the NSA about any changes in government policy and any other issues that may arise.

### Description
Previously marked with tensions over authority and resource allocation, there has been a concerted push towards greater collaboration between the FMS and the Government of Somalia. The IGAD-FMS relationship (at least at Permanent Secretary level) and will debate together with the NSA about any changes in government policy and any other issues that may arise.

The project will support the enhancement of coordination under output 3.1 and strengthen the capacity of actors both at FMS and IGAD to work more effectively as a team. # Mitigation Measure(s)

**FAO Somalia is monitoring on a regular basis the security situation at all levels (FGS, TNS and local levels). The security situation and lessons learned are shared with the project team and stakeholders of the project target regions and districts. The target districts have been selected to mitigate the security related risks during the project implementation. Al-Shabab is active in some parts of the project areas. FAO Somalia has a strong presence at all levels (FGS, TNS and local levels) with its field offices in Mogadishu, Hargeisa, Baidoa, Kismayo, Jowhar, and Garowe. The field offices are staffed by Somali nationals supporting these regions with a strong field presence (FAO Somalia has the main office in Mogadishu and the field offices) but also have local partners such as the National Disaster Management Authority (NEMA), the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, and the Ministry of Water and Irrigation. FAO Somalia is working closely with these institutions to ensure that the project works with the ministries at FGS level as well as at TNS/Local levels which have a strong presence and better access to local communities than the national government.**

The risk mitigation measures involve different approaches and tools, and interventions are designed based on the last 30 years of experience in Somalia. The FAO Somalia has been working with the Somali Government and other stakeholders for many years past and continued in sectoral strategies. FAO adopted the design and model of implementation of activities to mitigate risks or reduce their impact by using a combination of technical, institutional and financial measures. These measures include: (i) establishment of a degree of accessibility; (ii) distribution of inputs via a network of verified and vetted local agro-dealers and suppliers; (iii) mobility of staff and equipment; (iv) use of digital tools (voice notes, video calls, GPS phones, etc.); (v) inclusion of contingency budget in case of security emergency, etc.

## Selected Risk Factor 3 Local resource-based tension or conflict

### Category
Technical and institutional

### Frequency
Medium

### Impact
Medium

#### Description
The Somali clan is clan-oriented and competition among clans underpins natural resource management. There has been also tension between pastoralists and farmers over land use, water resources and other natural resources. The inherent risk is assessed as high, but the residual risk is assessed as medium with the following mitigation measures.

## Mitigation Measures

The project will avoid areas where there are large-scale conflicts that have not yet been addressed or responded to by the project's mitigation measures. The project will build the capacity of communities in conflict management over natural resource management (NRM) and land use, including women and youth. This will be done through community dialogues and workshops on NRM and land use, which will also contribute towards building trust among groups. The project will conduct a series of participatory processes to identify and address the root causes of conflicts in the project areas. These include local dynamics, including conflict and peace divers over natural resources among local groups. This will support the project in tackling the underlying causes of conflict and promoting peaceful coexistence between communities. In addition, the project will also conduct sensitivity analysis which will also consider intra-territorial conflict potential to ensure that the project does not create conditions for conflicts or exacerbate existing ones. The project will also engage with local stakeholders through community dialogues and workshops on NRM and land use, including women and youth. This will be done through community dialogues and workshops on NRM and land use, which will also contribute towards building trust among groups and to develop joint solutions for the management of natural resources in a sustainable manner. The project will also engage with local stakeholders such as women and youth, who will be engaged as part of this conflict mitigation and peace building discovery process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 66

## Selected Risk Factor Category Exogenous risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description

Exogenous risk include: (i) climate risks, more extreme weather events negatively impacting all landscape restoration efforts; (ii) COVID-19 and other pandemics, which could lead to a reduction in the number of people available for project implementation, or restrictions on movement into operational districts and discontinuation of activities/implementation, and would threaten the project's investments; (iii) political instability, including civil unrest, which could result in high costs and delays. The probability is rated as medium-high, but the following mitigation measures would lower the risk to medium.

### Mitigation Measures

The project seeks to restore and enhance the services provided by ecosystem that support sustainable and resilient livelihoods. The project will support investments in sustainable landscape management including restoration and climate-resilient agriculture practices. The project will also ensure that all areas of the landscape are managed as a whole, with particular attention given to areas such as inundation zones; will be identified and the landscape management plan may also identify areas where physical interventions, e.g., construction of embankments, can be made. The project will also seek to reduce the risk of climate change by ensuring that all investments in land use planning and implementation are based on a sound understanding of the risks associated with climate change, including how they might emerge during project implementation, such as sudden increases in material prices, unexpected delays, or urgent repairs. These actions will lower the impact of risk from medium to low.

The project seeks to ensure that all investments in land use planning and implementation are based on a sound understanding of the risks associated with climate change, including how they might emerge during project implementation, such as sudden increases in material prices, unexpected delays, or urgent repairs. These actions will lower the impact of risk from medium to low.

## Category Probability Impact Description

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

FAO includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the Government of Somalia related to AMCFLT, as:

a) The Government shall comply and shall require all persons and entities involved in its activities under the Project to comply with the UN Security Council Resolution 1844 (2008) and any subsequent resolutions.

b) The Government confirms it has obtained sufficient understandings from all persons and entities involved in its activities under the Project that they will comply with the UN Security Council Resolution 1844 (2008) and any subsequent resolutions, as well as with the substantive objectives of the GFA's Policy on Prohibited Practices.

c) Compliance with the above requirements is subject to the Government maintaining a list of all persons and entities under its control or indirectly, to provide support to individuals or entities associated with terrorism, as included in the list maintained by the Security Council Committee established pursuant to Resolution 1844 (2008). The list shall be updated at least once every six months. Failure to comply with sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security Council. This provision may be modified if the Government provides a written statement that it will not be subject to such sanctions.

d) Compliance with the above requirements is subject to the Project being implemented in full compliance with the UN Security Council Resolution 2174 (2013), related to Somalia, and, based on the currently applicable 1844 Sanctions List. No exemptions are needed from the UN Security Council for this project.

e) FAO will, as per current procedures, assess their impact on project operations and inform accordingly GCF.

As part of its due diligence process, FAO will ensure that all persons and entities involved in its activities under the Project comply with the above requirements. In addition, FAO conducts due diligence processes to all implementing partners, contractors, and third parties (before entering into agreements). Verification of the compliance of these persons and entities is conducted through the GCF Platform's Compliance and Feedback Mechanism (CFFM) platform. The Risk Management Unit (RMU) is checked to ensure that they are not affiliated to terrorist groups. The RMU database allows the UN Security Council Resolution 1844 (2008) and any subsequent resolutions, as well as the list maintained by the Security Council Committee established pursuant to Resolution 1844 (2008). As part of its due diligence process, FAO will, if applicable be registered. Contracted entities, including NGOs are required to sign a declaration of non-affiliation with terrorist groups and comply with the UN Security Council resolutions. Failure to comply with sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security Council. This provision may be modified if the Government provides a written statement that it will not be subject to such sanctions.

## Selected Risk Factor 7 Post Distribution Aid Diversion

### Category Probability Impact Description

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

Somalia is plagued by a systemic PDM Distribution-Aid Diversion (PDMDA), primarily identified in IDP camps, but also spread across other areas. The PDMDA is characterized by the diversion of aid intended for humanitarian purposes to non-humanitarian uses. This includes the selection of beneficiaries where they are required (mainly by local community representatives) to pay registration fee at time of enrollment. In addition, there have been reports that some local authorities and/or local military commanders may act on behalf of other individuals or entities such as district or local military commander, in order to divert aid intended for humanitarian purposes. The PDMDA is a systemic problem which requires a multi-sectoral approach. Mitigation measures would lower the risk to low.


---
**Page 72**
---

FAO Somalia’s approach on risk of PDAD involves implementing rigorous monitoring and evaluation protocols, establishing robust accountability mechanisms, conducting thorough risk assessments, utilizing advanced technology for tracking and tracing aid distribution, and ensuring transparency in all operations.

By utilizing a combination of actions and tools, systematically embedded in activities, it effectively reduces the risk of PDAD. Such measures include: (i) Risk Assessment and Mitigation, (ii) Community communication and sensitization, (iii) Transparent Beneficiary Tracking and Monitoring, (iv) Robust Accountability Mechanisms, (v) Effective Coordination and Collaboration, (vi) Third-party monitoring and oversight, (vii) CPMI reporting and response mechanisms, (viii) Digital Solutions, and (ix) Data protection and data sharing agreements.

In addition to these measures, FAO Somalia also employs a mobile money-based approach which involves a two-layer targeting approach, and provision in the Mobile Money Operators contracts with post-distribution transactions monitoring services to flag PDAD. This includes: (i) Bulk SMS informing of entitlement and hotline number to 100% beneficiaries, (ii) Bulk SMS surveys reaching 100% of HHS, and (iii) Data sharing agreements.

FAO Somalia ensures also systematic involvement of the Government in PDAD matters when recovery is not feasible.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words; approximately 1.5 pages)

### 108. During preparation of the environmental and social management framework, a risk assessment was carried out against the project’s potential adverse impacts on biodiversity, land use, water resources, human health, livelihoods, gender, social, and environmental aspects, and identified priority design elements to proactively address these. A detailed description of this process is provided in Annex 6. The ESFMs are designed to be flexible, allowing for local to national authorities, relevant technical institutions, to NGOs and CSOs, among others, as outlined in Annex 7. As per the GCF’s policy on environmental and social risk assessment (ESRA), the ESFs are a key component of the ESFM. The ESFM (Annex 6) identifies the project as Category B (Moderate Risk), based on the fact that the project contains activities with significant adverse impacts, which are not fully mitigated or avoided in the project design and/or cumulatively, are generally site-specific, largely reversible, and mostly addressed through mitigation measures.

### 109. Overall, the project is expected to bring about major positive impacts, thanks to the social inclusion participatory processes that have been carried out with local communities, as well as the implementation of a number of measures resulting in Ecological Based Management (EBM) for biodiversity, soil and water conservation, land restoration, reforestation, and agro-ecology. The project will also contribute to the promotion of native or endemic species and breeds sourced from local or national markets where available.

### 110. The project is considered as Moderate Risk with outcomes of the application of FAO’s safeguards standard (see screening Table G.1.1).

## 198. Institutional arrangements: Overall compliance with the project’s ESM will be ensured by the Environmental Social Management Framework (ESMF), who will work closely together with the Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist (who will oversee the GAP).

In addition, there will be zero tolerance of sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment (SEAH), and the project will ensure that all staff are trained in SEAH. The project will also ensure that the project complies with the gender equality principles as outlined by the Gender Management Framework (GMF). The project will support gender sensitization and training for project staff and beneficiaries on gender equity and social inclusion issues, including the GMF. A Gender Inclusion Mechanism (GIM) will be developed to guide the project in its efforts to mainstream gender equality into all aspects of the project. Specific procedures to minimize SEAH risk will be developed for the project GIM, to ensure the mechanism is survivor-centered and includes a complaint handling procedure that ensures confidentiality and redress for anyone affected by SEAH.

### Gender Inclusion Mechanism (GIM):

The GIM is an integral project management element that intends to seek feedback from beneficiaries and resolve complaints on project activities and performance. The mechanism will be based on the principles of survivor-centeredness, accountability, transparency, and respect for mechanisms preferred by the local beneficiaries, FAO and EEs will inform communities about the GIM through culturally appropriate means. The GIM will be designed in a participatory manner (e.g., community-based, project-based), and field-level reforms mechanism and GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism). The CPU and FAO Sub-Office will ensure that all staff are trained on the GIM principles and procedures. The CPU will work with FAO Somalia’s competent officers to ensure the right application of GMF principles. Related SEAH and gender equality issues will be addressed through the GIM, which will be survivor-centered and include a complaint handling procedure that ensures confidentiality and redress for anyone affected by SEAH.

### G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words; approximately | page)

So far, Somalia has integrated gender equality objectives into its country’s constitution and national development plans. However, the progress in the implementation of the commitments is very slow. Women’s participation in the labor force remains low compared to men, with women having limited access to productive resources such as land on their mobility as a result of social norms. In the agriculture sector, women are actively engaged in crop and livestock production activities but they have limited access to inputs, credit facilities, markets, extension services and other income. Conflict and displacement have heightened the risk of SEAH and GBV against women and girls. Women’s rights to land ownership and inheritance remain a major challenge as well as the lack of awareness on gender equality norms. The impacts of climate change exacerbate existing gender inequalities.

The project will address these challenges by implementing measures that break down institutional barriers, mainstreaming gender equality and prevention of SEAH and GBV as an integrated element. The implementation of the GIM will be survivor-centered and include a complaint handling procedure that ensures confidentiality and redress for anyone affected by SEAH.

MINISTRY OF ENVIRONMENT & CLIMATE CHANGE

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)
Re: Funding proposal for the GCF to Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations (FAO) regarding Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled "Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia" as included in the funding proposal submitted by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) to us on April 28, 2024.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of H.E. Khalidyo Mohamed Almukhamed, the National Designee for Somalia.

Pursuant to GCF decision B/R/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objective, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Somalia has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Somalia.
(c) In accordance with relevant laws and regulations, including environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objective to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

## Secretariat's assessment of FP246

Proposal name: Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia (Dhigash)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

Country: Somalia

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Strong paradigm shift in transitioning from emergency humanitarian assistance to long-term developmental ones. Technically sound and with a good exit strategy, it will enhance the capacity of local farmers to adapt to climate impacts by addressing financial, institutional, and technical environment barriers - most importantly water, agricultural inputs and market access. The project is timely and relevant, post-conflict African State, affected by a multi-year drought and poor agricultural systems. Helping the agriculture sector to adapt to new climate patterns has an extremely high priority for improving the lives and livelihoods of the population of Somalia. | The project has a heightened risk profile owing fundamentally to external risk factors beyond the control of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations, including food security and risk mitigation strategies at various levels, from site selection to adaptive management measures and contingency budget provisions. |

The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations is an accredited entity in terms of presence in the field and experience gained through conflict periods in Somalia, building expertise on the ground, which may prove critical in sensitizing local communities.

The proposed project benefits from demonstrated commitment and alignment with national priorities, with foreseen participation of various layers of national and international stakeholders receiving vital capacity-building.


---
**Page 85**
---

1. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/J840/02.

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

* Somalia is one of the most fragile countries in the world from various points of view including poverty, conflict, displacement, conflicts over natural resources and economic development, which are exacerbated by climate change.
* Many households are food-insecure and 89 per cent of the population lives below the national poverty line (defined as a consumption-based International Food Security Phase Classification of 1.7 million calories below the age of five).
* The domestic production meets only 22 per cent of per capita cereal needs on average and the country is heavily dependent on imports to meet its food security requirements.
* Agricultural productivity is very low, with an estimated 80 per cent of the population depending on natural pastures on the country's vast rangelands. Agricultural productivity is very low and most farmers are small-scale subsistence farmers for major crops, including maize and sorghum, in an arid and semi-arid climate.
* There have been significant changes in the climate and extreme climatic events in Somalia over recent years. These include increased frequency of droughts, floods, and a chain of compounding and cascading impacts for communities and ecosystems. At the same time, despite the country's rich biodiversity and natural resources, there has been little investment in agriculture and inadequate access to climate information among last mile users are significant barriers that must be lifted for local communities to be able to leverage climate-resilient livelihoods.
* The project proposes a multi-faceted approach to address these barriers. It restores productive land through the restoration of degraded rangelands, promotes sustainable agricultural practices and increasing access to water, enhancing access to climate-resilient inputs (seeds, feed, fodder), improving market access for producers and strengthening institutions and governance structures in order to support the transition towards climate-resilient agriculture.
* The project aims to achieve three outcomes (see section 2.2 below) that work together to address these barriers: on one hand, they are supported by the restoration of the productive landscape and, on the other hand, they are supported by stronger government institutions and governance structures in order to ensure their sustainability. These outcomes support the transition from unsustainable, unproductive and maladapted production systems towards climate-resilient agricultural practices.
* The number of people living below national poverty lines has declined because of rangeland degradation and the project will support the transition away from these livelihoods by providing access to best practices and lessons learned from recent projects and programmes and is closely connected with efforts to rebuild the country following years of conflict.

The project will directly benefit 1,031 people (L52,142 direct beneficiaries and 972,689 indirect beneficiaries). The project will directly contribute to the following targeted results in the context of the GCF Strategic Plan 2014–2027: (I) [Supporting the country's adaptation and mitigation goals]; (ii) [Food - (Ecosystems) - 1,800 hectares to be conserved, restored or improved]; (iii) [Water - (Ecosystems) - 3,500 hectares to be conserved, restored or improved]; (iv) [Energy - (Ecosystems) - 4,900 hectares to be conserved, restored or improved]; and targeted result 9. The project will also support dissemination of climate information to


---
**Page 86**

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Restored landscapes are resilient and sustainably managed (total cost: USD 684,530 per cent from GCF)

Activities under this component are designed to restore productive landscapes and facilitate ecological resilience, supporting ecosystem-based adaptation to climate change so that they can provide food security for the most vulnerable rural communities. This requires both the creation and the strengthening of local planning systems and the development of a resilient water supply system. Given the significant vulnerability of water scarcity as a limiting factor in development in Somalia, the project has opted to adopt the sub-watershed approach (component 2) as an enabling mechanism for this component.

Improved participatory landscape and natural resource management and governance are established through the creation of local planning systems that ensure that landscapes are restored and sustainably managed. And 13 capacities of local governments to plan, budget, implement and monitor for effective locally led adaptation are enhanced.

### Component 2: A sub-watershed approach (total cost: USD 418,764 GCF cost: USD 270,573 TAFI per cent co-finance: USD 146,191)

Activities under this component are designed to ensure that local communities derive increased benefits from the restoration of their landscapes. The project targets existing key value chains (maize, sesame, sorghum), which are climate-vulnerable staple crops, as well as livestock production systems in pastoral and agropastoral areas transitioning from extensive intensive cropping and from pastoralism to agropastoralism. Given that a significant proportion of the population is dependent on rainfed agriculture relying on a resilient water supply for agricultural production through a combination of ecosystem-based adaptation (component 1) and green and grey infrastructure solutions at both landscape and farm level, this component aims to ensure that the benefits from the restoration are secured and sustainably managed. 22 local communities enabled to practice locally specific climate-resilient agriculture and livestock management practices and generate income from sustainable natural resource management and climate-resilient value chains.

### Component 3: An improved institutional enabling environment for sustainable landscape management (total cost: USD 670,382 -100 per cent from GCF)

The objective of this component is the project's sustainability and upscaling strategy. The aim is to create conditions and an enabling environment for the achievement, replication and broader adoption of project results. Given the fragile conditions of the Government of Somalia, strengthening institutional capacity is a key element in ensuring that the project can establish new norms for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture. The activities support both the implementation of the upscaling strategy (output 3.1 considers legal frameworks, enforcement modalities and coordination [the normative elements of institutional capacity]), while output 3.2 strengthens the informational capacity of the


---
**Page 87**
