### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

The Secretariat considers the proposal to be strongly aligned with GCF investment criteria, particularly in terms of impact and paradigm shift potential, as well as needs of the recipient.

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

- The project shows strong impact potential, reaching an estimated **1,152,142 direct beneficiaries**, or per cent of the population of Somalia, mostly in the form of improved water management and irrigation systems.
- Improved access to water and increased yields from rainfed agriculture are critical at the farm level to improve food security and livelihoods in Somalia, particularly for poor rural households.
- In partnership with farmers and agrarian-based field schools is ambitious in its scale, with 420 field schools benefiting up to **85,932 people** (including household members) as facilitators of knowledge transfer. These participants help to build up farmer and herder technical knowledge on resilient agriculture.
- Climate-resilient agricultural practices and increased access to water are expected to result in the production of 10,476 metric tons of improved wheat, 25,389 metric tons of sesame, raising household incomes by **18–66 per cent** and contributing to food security in the area, providing a keenly needed benefit to over **2.1 million people (direct and indirect beneficiaries)**.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift
**Scale: Medium to high**

Under the current agro-pastoral system, Somalia has undergone successive shocks as a result of land degradation and overgrazing, compounded by increasing frequency and intensity of droughts, leading to the failure of the rangeland ecosystems upon which households depend.

The proposed project is designed to be transformative and sufficiently ambitious to ensure food security under climate change scenarios. The approach proposed combines infrastructure investment, rural finance and value addition, so that households can exit the survival mode and enter a more sustainable livelihood system. This will provide opportunities for diversification and builds social safety nets. This would be enabled by an integrated approach targeting the capacity-building of both producers and support systems


---
**Page 88**
---

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

The project, as a result of its ecosystem regeneration using indigenous species, Prosopis removal will provide an estimated annual benefit of $14,000 hectares, with significant environmental co-benefits. These include improvements in biodiversity, reduced erosion and flooding risk, increased soil fertility, and enhanced carbon sequestration, which also contribute to increasing natural land productivity. It will further lead to reductions in greenhouse gas emissions from the land-use sector, which have been estimated at 1.68 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent per year.

Similarly, social and economic benefits are highly relevant in the low development context of the rural areas. These include income generation through improved food and production security, enabling a steady income gradient for small-scale commercialization for rural households, enabling coping strategies to deal with extreme events, and improved access to credit - which, combined with other interventions, will contribute to poverty reduction. The project proposal will support job creation, promote the engagement of youth, women and minorities in value chain activities, and ultimately strengthen social cohesiveness and contribute to the prevention of conflict over natural resources.

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: High

The project targets one of the most climate-vulnerable countries globally. Somalia has the highest Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative vulnerability score and is currently affected by a multi-dimensional crisis that exacerbates already high levels of food insecurity and exacerbated rural poverty, two of the key focuses of the project.

Somalia is affected by a long conflict that has led to severe underinvestment in rural infrastructure and services. The country also faces significant challenges related to governance, as an undeveloped private sector, both of which are underlying factors in the food crisis in Somalia. The proposal recaptures these and tackles them through a robust and integrated set of activities.

The resources of the Government of Somalia are also severely limited, relying largely on international assistance with access to credit at scale for the primary sector, making GOI grant support a necessity.

## Country ownership

### Scale: High

The proposal shows strong alignment with current government policies and programmes to foster adaptive capacity and climate-resilient development, including the nationally determined contribution, national adaptation programme of action, the National Development Plan, and the National Climate Change Strategy. The project also aligns with priority actions contained under one of the nationally determined contribution's focus areas, "Climate smart agriculture, Livestock and Land use" (1) Provision of climate-resilient water resources


---
**Page 89**
---

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The overall assessment of this project’s efficiency and effectiveness is medium to high.

The AE developed a high-quality robust economic and financial analysis with clear and thoroughly referenced data and assumptions. The economic and financial analysis evaluates the economic viability of the project under different climate scenarios, including climate-resilient agriculture packages in each of the project intervention zones. It includes both baseline and alternative scenarios for the same project under different climate scenarios (Representative Concentration Pathway 2.6 and 8.5).

Financial viability in the long run is demonstrated through the profitability indicators for the project. In the baseline scenario, the project generates returns above the financial discount rate of 15% cost with a net present value (NPV) of USD 30 million and internal rate of return (IRR) of 28%. In the alternative scenario, the project generates returns at an NPV of USD 34 million. The results remain robust to the sensitivity analysis conducted on the decrease in agricultural productivity due to climate change. It is important to note that the without-the-project scenario is also financially viable, and farmers may opt for other adaptation strategies such as diversification or migration.

The project’s financial adequacy is demonstrated through the ability of the concessionaire GCF to make the project viable. The financial adequacy and appropriateness of this concessionality are partly due to the fact that smallholder farmers in Somalia predominantly rely on cash transactions and have limited access to finance in the agricultural sector, with less than 10 per cent holding bank accounts or having access to credit facilities. This is also reflected by the low levels in the form of grants rather than debt instruments. Therefore, the justification for full concessionality is strong.

The project’s adaptation cost is USD 69 per direct beneficiary and USD 38 per total beneficiary. These costs are at the lower end of the GCF portfolio for climate-resilient agriculture projects. The AE has developed a detailed implementation plan that incorporates best practices in the project, specifically leveraging the expertise of the AE and experience in delivering similar projects such as the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF) Somalia Agriculture through field schools. However, the potential for catalyzing and leveraging private and public sector investments is not associated with quantitative estimates, and the degree of financial innovation is also not shown.

### Environmental and social safeguards

#### Project overview
The project is structured around three main components that aim to enhance the resilience of agriculture through sustainable landscape management and improved irrigation water supply, promote climate-resilient livelihoods in agriculture and livestock production, and enable environments conducive to sustainable landscape management. The project also aims at achieving significant environmental and social co-benefits including reduced greenhouse gas emissions from the agriculture, forestry and other land use sector.

#### Environmental and social (E&S) risk category and safeguard instrument
The project has been screened using the FAO Project Environmental and Social Screening Checklist, giving a preliminary assessment of E&S risks. The project is categorized as having low E&S risk categorization system. This categorisation indicates that the project will have activities with potential environmental or social impacts which are unlikely to be significant individually or cumulatively, will be few, generally site-specific, largely reversible and readily addressed through standard mitigation measures. As a result of this screening, no specific projects to be implemented in several regions. These activities/subprojects will be based on master plans called the “Regional Development Plans” (RDPs) which are being developed by the project stakeholders during implementation. For these reasons, an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) is deemed the appropriate pre-project instrument for the project. The ESMF will be prepared in consultation with relevant stakeholders and prepared during implementation.

#### Compliance with IFC’s environmental and social safeguards (E&S) standards
The paragraphs below describe how the project will comply with the standards.
- **ESM 1: assessment and management of E&S risks and impacts**. The ESMF includes a project context, description of the project activities/subprojects, details of stakeholder engagement, an indigenous peoples planning framework, and details of expected impacts, mitigation measures, procedures, and implementation arrangements. The ESMF also identifies key environmental and social issues that may arise from the project and appropriate mitigation measures. Further, the ESMF contains various frameworks, templates and outlines of management plans for each activity/subproject. The project team will ensure that each subject/project further conduct an E&S impact assessment on the proposed activities in the region/subject area. This will be followed by preparation of a regional or subject management plan. Project subactivities will undergo a screening, assessment, review and clearance process before their implementation.
- **ESM 2: labour and working conditions**. The ESMF provides a brief background on the current policies and legislation on labour and working conditions in Somalia, including international conventions and standards that apply to the project. It also identifies potential risks and potential negative impacts relating to labour and working conditions: (i) risks of child and forced labour; (ii) risks associated with the use of temporary or seasonal workers at construction sites; and (iii) poor working conditions. The ESMF provides measures to mitigate the risks, such as the preparation of a labour management plan and an occupational health and safety plan. Wherever necessary, a separate subproject will be prepared for each activity/subproject based on the nature of the work involved. Subactivities/subjects/projects will be required to prepare and implement their own labour management plans and occupational health and safety plans. It has also been prepared that identifies and addresses some occupational health and safety issues.
- **ESM 3: resource efficiency and pollution prevention**. The project will have temporary impacts on the environment from construction activities, including dust generation as expected from the activities/subprojects that will involve construction work, solid and domestic waste from offices and other facilities, and water use for irrigation purposes only. Other pollution impacts may occur from activities that involve support to the agricultural and


---
**Page 91**
---

# ESS-4: Community health, safety and security (CHSS)

The anticipated CHSS risks for this project include:

(i) construction site and route safety hazards;
(ii) increases in criminality - including theft of equipment and materials, as well as increased violence (GBV) - due to a possible influx of workers into local communities; 
(iii) spread of communicable diseases among the displaced population;
(iv) potential increase in transmission vectors of vector-borne diseases due to changes in the landscape;
(v) exposure to hazardous substances, including chemicals and pesticides used for agricultural activities that involve production and distribution of food products; and
(vi) potential exacerbation of existing CHSS risks related to conflict situations.

The project will follow recommendations established in industry-specific best practices for management of risks related to construction and value chain operations. The project will ensure compliance with national legal requirements and good international practice, and will ensure inclusive engagement to avoid forced evictions and displacement of communities. The project will also ensure that hazardous project funds be used to procure pesticides. Moreover, highly hazardous pesticides will not be used by the project. The project will promote integrated pest management (IPM) practices to reduce the use of and dependency on agrochemicals, and will employ sustainable soil and water management.

Regarding potential exacerbation of the conflict situation, the EPMF provides a comprehensive approach for addressing CHSS risks related to conflict situations in fragile and conflict-affected settings. The project will apply this approach by:

(a) engaging with relevant stakeholders at all levels;
(b) ensuring that the project does not contribute to displacement or loss of livelihoods; 
(c) developing appropriate mitigation measures, including land tenure arrangements as part of the land-opportunity assessment; and
(d) developing sustainable land and water management frameworks.

# ESS-5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project will use the FAO Voluntary Guidelines on the Responsible Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of National Food Security and incorporate land tenure assessment as part of the land-opportunity assessment. The project will also ensure that no land is acquired through involuntary resettlement or displacement of people or communities, and resources from the project are not used to acquire land for private purposes. In addition, no set-aside land or additional conservation areas to be allocated as part of the project.

Reforestation/rehabilitation areas will be located mainly on public or community land only.


---
**Page 92**
---

**(executed by regional administrations) or interventions on private land executed by communities with funding from the project). The proposed activities mostly involve the involvement of districts on a purely voluntary and demand-driven basis. As added safeguards against potential negative impacts on biodiversity, the project will implement the following under the project: (i) activities that involve relocation and/or demolition of any permanent house or business; (ii) activities that involve the construction of new houses for local people; (iii) improvement in the resettlement of local people and villages/communities; (iv) activities that involve land acquisition; and (v) activities that involve the establishment of new settlements or the expansion of existing settlements.**

### GCF B.05/92/Add.08

Page 13

#### ES: 6 biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project will implement a biodiversity conservation and sustainable management plan for the project area and identifies potential impacts and risks of project activities involved. The project will not involve land conversion or expansion of agriculture frontiers, clearing of native forest or similar activities that may have significant negative impacts on biodiversity. Instead, the project will use mainly local/native breeds and species sourced from local or national markets and/or conservation areas. All livestock and poultry rearing materials (breeding materials, species and breed) will be free from pests and diseases. Furthermore, the non-native species introduced into the project area will not have a significant negative impact on land within natural habitats and existing or proposed protected areas; (ii) introduction of invasive species (species that are not native to the project area); (iii) conversion of agricultural land for climate-resilient and are known for their non-invasive properties; and (iii) significant conversion or degradation of natural habitats, or activities where the conservation and/or environmental impact assessment is inadequate. The biodiversity management plan and country planning framework (BMPF) has been prepared that provides detailed descriptions of the project area and its biodiversity status, including a description of the project area and an assessment of the impacts of project activities on biodiversity and their management measures. The BMPF will be used to identify potential negative impacts on biodiversity, particularly component 1. The potential negative impacts on biodiversity may come mainly from conversion or degradation of natural habitats, introduction of invasive species, and value chain development. Infrastructure may cause habitat fragmentation and loss of high-value ecosystems, while the pursuit of livelihoods and value-chain development may ignore agricultural practices that are harmful to biodiversity. To address these issues and to address these impacts, the BMPF seeks to integrate biodiversity conservation objectives into the formulation of project activities. The BMPF will be used as a basis for conducting biodiversity baseline assessments to inform the LMPs; (b) biodiversity impact assessment of water and the market for livestock and poultry products; (c) establishment of local community-based natural resource management committees, water authorities, water users, the MoA, producer groups, and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) on biodiversity conservation, habitat restoration and ecosystem functions.

#### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES standard 7 (Indigenous Peoples)

An Indigenous Peoples’ Policy has been developed for this project. The policy acknowledges the diverse nature of the nation, as well as the complex relationships between indigenous peoples and the state. It also recognizes that indigenous peoples are key to social inclusion and safeguarding of rights for all marginalized and excluded communities. A stakeholder identification mission will identify stakeholder groups and project beneficiaries, including indigenous peoples. The Indigenous Peoples’ Policy will provide a description of the project, its activities, potential benefits and risks; and will be provided in the local language and participate in consultations with indigenous peoples to ensure that their concerns and priorities related to participation in the project, in a manner that is conflict-sensitive. Iterative discussions will be held between the project team and indigenous peoples to ensure that all concerns are identified early on. A free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) procedure will be carried out and capacity-building will be informed by local consultations. The All Highlights

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF SEAH requirements.

Despite the development of the federal- and state-level national gender policies and other efforts, progress on implementation and resource allocation towards gender equity efforts has been slow. Women in rural areas are particularly vulnerable to climate change owing to their reliance on natural resources for their livelihoods. They are at the front line continuing to bear the brunt of climate change impacts. Women have limited capacity to respond to the challenges, having limited access to economic resources, land ownership and decision-making processes. They are disproportionately affected by gender-based


---
**Page 94**
---

discrimination, climate change impacts and high poverty rates, they also bear increased responsibilities as heads of households and are excluded from decision-making spaces and leadership roles. Additionally, they have limited access to education, healthcare, training opportunities and financial services. The lack of representation in government structures and infrastructure. GRV, unpaid care work and a lack of representation in consultation meetings and management committees further exacerbate their marginalization.

The project will address these challenges through a range of activities aimed at empowering women and promoting gender equality. Key initiatives include increasing women's access to education and training opportunities; providing financial services for women; improving their representation in committees and providing gender-sensitive training in agriculture and leadership. The project will also focus on ensuring that women have access to basic literacy, vocational skills development and economic support. Additionally, it will address GRV and discrimination through awareness-raising campaigns and support services, promote women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels of government; provide access to healthcare, education and essential resources. Advocacy for policy changes to address gender inequalities and ensure that women have equal opportunities and rights is also a key aspect.

The gender action plan defines activities, indicators and targets for women’s participation and benefits with corresponding timelines, budget and gender expertise.


### 4.3 Risks

1. The proposed project exhibits high risks of political instability, insecurity impacting access, and local resource-based conflict, making it essential to enhance political engagement, provide security and ensure the safety of staff. This will be achieved through enhanced physical monitoring and decentralized risk reporting. The AE proposes several mitigation measures and the Secretariat has developed a comprehensive contingency plan for various risk scenarios before or during the implementation stage.

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

2. The project operates in a high-risk environment characterized by a high turnover of government officers and potential shifts in development priorities, which complicates long-term planning and resource allocation. Mitigation measures include aligning project priorities with the national strategies of Somalia and integrating them into the AE, and engaging high-level government structures to manage policy changes proactively.

#### 4.3.2 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

3. The project in Somalia is subject to risks due to ongoing political and security challenges. Mitigation plans are in place, utilizing the deep regional expertise of the AE and its group partners to ensure that project activities are implemented effectively. FAO has embedded multiple risk mitigation strategies at various levels, from site selection and enhanced local collaboration with government counterparts to contingency planning for delays or disruptions during implementation, as well as contingency budget provisions, which are included in the time schedule.

#### 4.3.3 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

4. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.


---
**Page 95**
---

### 4.3.1 Compliance risk (medium risk)

The project will be executed by the AE, EEO and the Government of Somalia, acting through Modi and MeDG in the MoU. The project activities generally present relatively lower risks of money-laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF). However, the AE highlights that the operating environment is complex and subject to change, which could increase overall ML/TF risk during project implementation. Therefore, the AE has assessed ML/TF inherent risk as high. However, the AE considers the residual risk to be low on account of key mitigating measures in place, including the inclusion of a counter-party due diligence process for terrorism (AM/CFT) clauses in the project agreement with the Government of Somalia, as well as the due diligence and monitoring requirements under the MoU. The overall ML/TF implementation arrangements are further augmented by the inclusion of a central project implementation arrangement, which will be managed by the AE. This central project implementation arrangement, will be held by a Technical Advisor and National Technical Coordinator appointed by FAO, and will include a risk management plan. As such, key risks and control measures are identified and appropriate controls are effectively applied throughout the project cycle.

In addition, the AE assesses the risk of post-distribution aid diversion as high. To mitigate this risk, the AE has incorporated specific mitigation measures into the project, including the use of tracking and tracing technology, as well as post-distribution transaction monitoring. The AE has assessed that the combined effect of these measures will reduce ML/TF risks to low level.

Given the project-specific risks identified, the mitigation measures incorporated into the implementation plan, and the projections of the AE of ongoing external factors that could have a significant impact on ML/TF risks throughout the project cycle, the Secretariat determines that the overall risk remains high.


### 4.4 Fiduciary

As the AE, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are effectively managed to deliver results in line with the project objectives. The Secretariat has assessed that this includes the timeliness and quality of reporting to GCF; and (iv) project closure and evaluation. FAO will ensure that the project is executed according to the detailed pre-defined provisions set out in the Accreditation Master Agreement between AGF and GCF.

The project will be executed by FAO and the Government of Somalia acting through MeDG and MoU, respectively. As the executing agency (EA), FAO will manage all GCF proceeds. FAO will act as an EA and will ensure strong country-driven execution of project activities in line with the agreed project objectives. FAO will also be responsible for disbursing funds based on its comparative advantages. This will allow Somalia to benefit from the technical and operational expertise of FAO, while ensuring that the project is implemented efficiently by Somalia, while providing opportunities for the government to increase its capacity through technical assistance provided under the GCF. The AE will have overall responsibility over the three project components. FAO will also exercise the activities co-financed by FAO.

In order to fulfill the EA functions, FAO will set up a dedicated project task force in line with FAO’s internal procedures and guidelines. The task force will be led by the Project Leader/Handler, the Lead Technical Officer, the Funding Liaison Officer, the HQ Technical Officer and other technical officers, as appropriate, and will remain independent from the EE functions performed by FAO.

GCF proceeds received by FAO in its capacity as AE will flow to the EEs, namely FAO-Somalia, Modi and MeDG, for the implementation of project activities. The provisions

## Results monitoring and reporting

The project will establish a CPUL which will be functional for the entire duration and be responsible for the overall management of the project on behalf of the Federal Government and each federal member state. The main functions of the CPUL, following the guidance of the Project Steering Committee (PSC), are to: (i) provide strategic guidance for the project; (ii) ensure effective management, coordination, implementation and monitoring of the project through the effective implementation of the work plan; (iii) ensure that all project deliverables will be led and managed by a project-recruited Technical Advisor and National Technical Coordinator as per the roles defined by FAO and will be responsible for overall project management and coordination with project stakeholders. The Project Coordinator will be appointed by FAO and will be selected in accordance with relevant FAO Manual Sections.

A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will provide strategic guidance for the project. The PSC will be led by MoECC and co-chaired by FAO-Somalia. The CPUL will serve as Rapporteur to the committee. The role of the Project Steering Committee will be: (i) provide overall direction and oversight; (ii) review and approve project work plans, including resource constraints; (iii) address project issues as raised by the national Project Implementation Coordination Team (PICT); (iv) provide guidance on new project risks and agree on possible countermeasures and management actions to address these risks. The PSC will also be responsible for reviewing and approving project recommendations to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily according to plan; (v) review and approve the annual workplan and provide the necessary strategic guidance for its implementation, including the quality assessment rating report; (vi) make recommendations for subsequent resource allocation. The PSC will be supported by a Project Coordinator who will provide direction and advice for exceptional situations when the Project Coordinator's tolerances are exceeded or not met. The PSC will be chaired on behalf of FAO by a Senior Project Technical Committee and regional project technical committees.

Financial management and control and procurement processes as implemented per FAO Handbook, including the use of the enterprise-based enterprise resource planning system, the Global Resources Management System, in its offices worldwide; it provides all FAO employees with access to the system for their respective functions and responsibilities. Using this system improves the flow of financial information, supports financial management and control, and ensures compliance with FAO rules and regulations and internal controls. FAO maintains a chart of accounts which is used by the whole organization and that allows for a systematic recording of all transactions in terms of projects and supporters and provides a standardized ordering structure that enables data to be retrieved and reported on as required. The system also facilitates the preparation of reports and summaries to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements.

All project activities will be monitored, evaluated and reported by the Project Coordinator and CoEs and will assume fiduciary responsibility in accordance with FAO standards and procedures. In addition to the above responsibilities, the Project Coordinator is responsible for compliance with FAO rules and regulations, which are contained and detailed in the FAO Handbook (including those related to financial monitoring, audit and procurement). During implementation, FAO will provide technical assistance as required by the Project Coordinator.

This may include monitoring missions, spot checks and participation at project governance meetings. The project will be audited internally and externally following FAO financial regulations.

## The funding proposal is based on a sound theory of change and is linked to a logical framework allowing the project to measure results and evaluate its impact. The monitoring and evaluation (M&E) plan submitted by the AE lays out the data collection and measurement tools to be deployed, as well as the methods for assessing the achievement of the components of the Integrated Results Management Framework and the GCF Evaluation Policy.

### 6.7 The project's theory of change illustrates three distinct and complementary outcomes that target different levels of government and stakeholders in the climate-resilient environment for national and local institutions. The sustainable landscape management and livelihoods improvement activities are expected to contribute to a more resilient ecosystem services system, which provides a multi-pronged approach to enhance the resilience of rural communities and ecosystems in the targeted regions. Logical pathways from activities to outputs and outcomes are clear and supported by tangible indicators that can be measured at each level that allow the achievement of project outputs and outcomes are noted, with the most important being the overall impact on climate-resilient livelihoods.

### 6.8 The logical framework is linked with the climate adaptation outcomes and project outputs/outcomes as well as the GCF’s three most important outcome indicators such as direct and indirect beneficiaries reached (core indicator 2), value of physical assets made more resilient to climate change impacts (core indicator 3) and the extent to which resources brought under improved climate-resilient management practices (core indicator 4). Allow the measurement of adaptation impact at each level of the results chain. The logical framework on climate-resilient livelihoods and water security will measure the depth of the respective adaptation activities, while the logical framework for ecosystem services will use specific indicators have also been proposed to measure results at every level of the results chain.

### 6.9 The M&E plan gives a high-level understanding of how the various M&E-related activities will be conducted in the project and the roles and responsibilities of various actors in collecting data, monitoring risks and results, and quality assurance. In addition to the annual reporting requirements as outlined above for annual reporting, the entity will conduct midterm and endline beneficiary surveys to measure adaptation impact results. The AE will also organize a baseline survey in the first year to inform the logical framework on climate-resilient livelihoods and water security. No separate budget is set aside for conducting independent project evaluations at this scale and as per the GCF Evaluation Policy.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP246

Proposal name: Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia (Elaigada)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

Country: Somalia

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This is the first submission of the funding proposal for the programme titled “Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia” under the standard proposal approval process. The assessment by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) was conducted on 7 July 2024, and the report presented to the ITAP on 19 July 2024, slightly revised on 8 August 2024.* and informed by a set of written comments received from the Government of Somalia (GoS), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the accredited entity, the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), at 7 August 2024.

* The accredited entity requests a grant contribution from GCF of USD 79,787,264. The full project budget amounts to USD 94,901,497 and includes a co-financing contribution from FAO of USD 35,194,229.

* The implementing partners are FAO (i.e., FAO Somalia) and the Government of Somalia, acting through the Ministry of Environment and Climate Change and the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation.

* The project helps communities to manage resources and restore degraded landscapes, with the support of strengthened government extension services and climate information systems. It is expected to:

### Outcome 1: Restored landscapes are resilient and sustainably managed.
The project seeks to restore productive landscapes (watersheds) and facilitate ecological multifunctional land use through a landscape approach. The project aims to provide crucial ecosystem services and sustain the agricultural livelihoods of local communities.

### Outcome 2: Local livelihoods are resilient to climate change.
Targeting key value chains, the project will support the development of climate-resilient livelihood systems that ensure local communities derive increased and improved climate-resilient livelihood benefits.

### Outcome 3: An improved institutional enabling environment for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture is in place at the state and district levels. The project will contribute to the development and implementation of new norms for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture.


---
**Page 100**
---

5. The ITAP has assessed the funding proposal against the six GCF investment criteria.

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

This project is intended to contribute to all four GCF adaptation results areas (ARAs) in order of priority within these ARAs: ARA 2 - Climate-resilient food security; ARA 3 - Most vulnerable people and communities; ARA 4 - Infrastructure and built environment.

The project outcomes relating to food and water security stand to directly and indirectly benefit more than 2 million people across Somalia, many of whom have a recent history of living with conflict. The application of climate-resilient agricultural practices and agroforestry methods is expected to cover an area of 418,00 hectares.

- Agricultural diversification: the introduction of drought-tolerant crops such as teff, sorghum and sesame from the application of climate-resilient agricultural practices and improved access to water. The project should also improve the efficiency of livestock production, for example through better feed management and improved grazing systems.
- Strengthening institutional capacity and regional systems: the project will contribute to increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making, especially at the local level.

With a well-designed project in a challenging environment, the impact potential - strengthening adaptive capacity and reducing exposure to climate risks - is assessed as medium to high.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The project’s theory of change statement suggests that “If local communities’ capacity is strengthened to implement sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture and in conjunction with the development of a market for their products, then the livelihoods of communities and ecosystems to climate change will be enhanced, because livelihoods will be derived from diversified agricultural systems which are more resilient and supported by adequate enabling environment.”

The project is likely to catalyse impact beyond a sole investment. The project’s logic and implementation strategy are coherent - in support of farming and pastoral communities as well as authorities at the state and federal level - is convincing and coherent with the policy framework.

Substantively, the project addresses the degradation of rangelands and the decline of pastoral systems by way of promoting a transformation of pastoral systems into agropastoral systems. The project will also contribute to the development of an irrigation system to facilitate access to markets and irrigation technologies, and with the relevant authorities to promote water use efficiency.

A system of water fees is to be developed to maintain upgraded or rebuilt irrigation infrastructures. Albeit a challenge in itself, such an arrangement stands to increase the chances


---
**Page 101**
---

The project works with crop and livestock producers to simultaneously restore the ecological resilience and the economic productivity of degraded landscapes. Parts of Somalia are under “terminal” degradation, where land has been rendered unproductive due to high water demand. Under such circumstances, degradation may still be halted and productivity increased through a combination of soil conservation practices (e.g., terracing) and reduced water demand (e.g. through multi-cropping or shading) and by enhancing soil quality.

The project also seeks to form part of a broader shift away from Somalia’s present dependence on staple crops such as sorghum, which are vulnerable to drought in early response and resilience building. FAO reporting suggests that farm-level disaster preparedness indices are low (10%) with only 25% of farmers having access to insurance for their farms.

With a capacity-enhancing project in a challenging environment, the paradigm shift potential - diversifying livelihoods to be more climate resilient – is assessed as medium to high. 

### Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

The proposed project should yield substantial sustainable development benefits in several dimensions:

- **Environmental**: Environmental benefits from the implementation of landscape management plans include the regeneration of the natural land cover through reforestation, agroforestry and rangeland rehabilitation. Biodiversity stands to be enhanced by using local species and removing invasive species.
- **Landscapes**: Landscape organization - around sub-watersheds – should also contribute to environmental benefits such as improved water retention in the soil, increased soil fertility and soil moisture, with greater water availability. This is likely to increase land productivity and reduce emissions from the land-use sector providing both economic and environmental benefits.
- **Social and Economic**: Social and economic co-benefits would also include the employment generated by farm-level activities, food processing and the nursery.
- **Food Security**: Food security can be improved through increased yields, allowing more to be spent on food. This is of critical importance across Somalia, and particularly in conflict areas where 1.2 million people face acute food insecurity, and some 1.7 million young children suffer from acute malnutrition.
- **Local Area Social Benefits**: Local area social co-benefits can also be derived from local institution-building in the form of water user associations and landscape management committees. The project involves a participatory gender-sensitive approach and pays attention to gender parity and the inclusion of the most marginalized.
- **Gender Inclusion**: The participation of women as decision makers and agents of change is fostered through the project’s gender action plan. This includes recognizing and addressing the different needs and barriers women currently face in accessing land, productive assets, education, information and services.

FAO 2013: The Impact of Droughts on Agriculture and Food Security 2013: Reducing and reducing risks through resilience building in resilient. Available at: <http://www.fao.org/3/a-i6895e.pdf>. Retrieved 24 June 2017.


---
**Page 102**
---

## Needs of the recipients Scale: High

26. Somalia is one of Africa’s most fragile countries in the world, facing rampant inequality, displacement and conflict, all worsened by limited economic opportunities and escalating climate change. The effects of recent heavy rainfall and flooding across Somalia are exacerbated by the limited capacity to absorb shocks.

27. The gender assessment and project-level action plan (Annex B to the funding proposal), referring to Somalia’s national adaptation programme of action, describes three groups [families of pastoralists, families of agro-pastoralists and families of sedentary communities] as vulnerable to climate change: Pastoralist depend on rangeland grazing for their livelihood and have very few options in terms of land tenure security. Sedentary communities are increasingly displaced persons face limited livelihood opportunities and heightened pressures on natural resources. Agro-pastoralists are also affected by the same factors, but they are further divided by gender division of labour, their unequal access to resources, low level of literacy and limited participation in decision-making.

28. Gender relations on the project sites are characterized by clan systems with a strong patriarchal culture. Women and girls have limited rights under customary laws, and are often excluded from decision making processes.

29. Smallholder farmers in the sorghum, maize, sesame and fodder value chains face similar challenges – lacking inputs such as improved certified seeds, fertilizers and mechanization services to increase productivity and income generation.

30. Women farmers face additional barriers relating to land access and tenure insecurity, as land is inherited through patrilineal lines. Women can be allocated land to grow fodder and vegetables but are not allowed to own or inherit it. This limits their ability to generate financial credit.

31. The project’s gender action plan recognises the limited livelihood opportunities and discriminating existing women in the targeting and selection of beneficiaries, the project seeks to include women from minority clans or ethnic groups in consultation activities. The project will also provide training on gender equality and empowerment for staff, as well as good practices to improve women's access to advisory services and develop a gender strategy with an agreed action plan.

32. The project’s analysis and endavours to overcome inequality and deprivation are commended. The needs of the recipient investment criterion is assessed as high.


## Country ownership Scale: Medium to high

33. Somalia grapples with a fragile political system, insecurity, poverty and climate change related shocks. Its 2021 nationally determined contribution[1] points to the contradiction that Somalia has little historical or current responsibility for global climate change, but nonetheless


---
**Page 103**
---

suffers disproportionately from the climate crisis. The effects in Somalia include droughts, floods and locust infestations resulting in loss of livestock and agricultural production.

34. The project aligns with Somali policies relevant to climate change and natural resources management. The fostering of adaptive capacity and climate-resilient livelihoods fits neatly within the framework of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) and its draft update of the nationally determined contribution (see funding proposal, para. 180).

35. The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach by way of providing local farmers with training and support, and supporting relevant authorities in coordinating and improving the enabling environment for climate-smart agriculture and greening land use.

36. The accredited entity, FAO, has an important presence in Somalia with over 400 staff and its country office located in Mogadishu, the capital of the Republic of Somalia. With a portfolio of 76 programmes, the organization works towards the transformation of agriculture in Somalia.

37. The programme is to be executed by FAO Somalia and the Government of Somalia, as follows:

(a) FAO, according to the funding proposal (para. 120), will restore strong country-driven elements of project activities*, allowing Somalia to *benefit from the technical and operations experience of a specialized development assistance agency from the United Nations.

(b) The project also provides opportunities for the Government of Somalia to increase its capacity through technical assistance and development and implementation of activities. The government of Somalia is represented at the level of the FAO accredited entity, which is also the national designated authority, and the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation; and

(c) As emphasized in the call between the TAC and the accredited entity, state governments and the Ministers' representation at the state level will play an important role in project implementation.

38. A Central Project Implementation Unit will be established for the day-to-day project implementation, with FAO support offices in each member state. FAO will appoint a Technical Advisor to provide technical assistance and guidance on project implementation, with guidance from a Project Steering Committee and Project Technical Committee.

39. Effective engagement with communities is necessary to ensure that land-use plans are acceptable to all stakeholders and that the project contributes to building trust among them, as well as trust in authorities and inefficient mechanisms for public participation. International organizations have a role to play in supporting governments in strengthening their structures and build local capacities. As state structures are recovering, developing integrated landscape-level natural resource management strategies is essential for promoting climate-resilient sustainable development and as a way of preventing further conflict.*

40. During project design, stakeholder consultations were conducted at various levels: (i) at the federal level with the Government of Somalia; (ii) at the regional level with international partners; (iii) in target federal member states with government and non-governmental organizations; (iv) at the district level with local authorities and communities; and (v) at the district level, with local authorities and communities (annex 7, para. 2). Community- and institution-level consultations are meticulously reported upon in the documentation constituting annexes 7.

*See https://www.fao.org/Somalia/en/

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium

- The funding proposal (para. 185) estimates the core beneficiary for the GCF grant at USD49.6 million, of which USD20.3 million is allocated to the project itself and USD29.3 million for all beneficiaries combined, and for total funding at USD 47.2 billion and USD 44.6 billion respectively.
- Two economic and financial modeling perspectives were appraised using Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) of 2.6 and 8.5. The results show how the results might change under differing climate hardcaps. Calculated over 20 years, using a conservative discount rate of 15 per cent, it is suggested that the expected benefits will outweigh the costs.
- The accredited entity is providing cash co-financing at the level of 16 per cent of the total budget, which is in line with the criteria set by the United States Agency for International Development to be implemented by FAO Somalia.
- The largest project component, outcome 2 (local livelihoods are resilient to climate change through access to water and irrigation infrastructure) goes to winning access to water and irrigation infrastructure. The second-largest component, outcome 1 (restored landscape productivity and sustainability managed) absorbs 36 per cent of the budget, dedicated to landscape restoration.
- Project component 7, outcome 2 (enabling environment for sustainable land management and climate-resilient agriculture) at both national and federal level), absorbs a lesser share of the budget at 6 per cent. It is hoped that the important capacity building and institutional strengthening will be carried out in close follow-up on the enabling environment to ensure the sustainability of the project's endeavours.
- As emphasized in the funding proposal (executive summary): "Given the fragile conditions, it is essential for the GCF to support a flexible approach. It is expected that Federal Member States can implement new norms for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture through dialogue and consensus-building rather than enforcement as initially worded", monitoring and coordination, and output 3.2 strengthen the capacity of the project entity to generate and disseminate useful, relevant and timely climate information to stakeholders.
- Project management absorbs a per cent of the budget, and monitoring and evaluation 5 per cent (the latter is not yet defined in detail). This is to support the adaptive management of the project endeavours. The adaptive project management approach is important given the risks and challenges of the project environment, including a fragile institutional context.
- The project seeks to build on best practices and lessons learned from recent projects and programs that have been implemented by FAO Somalia in the last 10 years, avoiding years of conflict. The project draws on a range of climate adaptation technologies, many of ecosystem-based, which should have the potential to be appropriately and iteratively developed through participatory/stakeholder engagement coupled with technical expertise provided by the Government of Somalia.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP finds that the proposed project, "Climate Resilient Agriculture in Somalia", is well-designed and aligns with the GCF Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024-2027 by accelerating climate action for the most vulnerable.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Part 1. Introduction

## Objective of the project, country, and target areas.

### Climate Change in Somalia: The Horn of Africa (Somalia, Kenya, Uganda, Ethiopia, Djibouti, Eritrea)

#### 1. Climate Change in Somalia
- **Impact on Livelihoods**: Climate change has led to prolonged and recurrent droughts in Somalia.
- **Economic Consequences**: These shocks have had a low capacity to absorb, respond, mitigate, cope, adapt to climate shocks and conflicts.

### Impact on Women
- **Women's Vulnerability**: Women and children constitute over 80 per cent of internally displaced persons (IDPs) who are particularly vulnerable to shocks.
- **Economic Opportunities**: Women have limited access to economic opportunities compared to men in the country.

### Impact on Agriculture
- **Climate Change Effects**: The climate is projected to become drier, warmer, more erratic and extreme with grave impacts on livestock crops, forestry and fisheries-based livelihoods.
- **Cumulative Impacts**: Cumulative impacts of climate change are compounded by conflict, displacement and control over land and decision-making power.

### Project Objectives
1. **Restored Landscapes Resilient to Climate Change**: Restoring landscapes that are resilient and sustainable agriculture systems.
2. **Improved Institutional Environment**: Creating an improved institutional environment for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture in the target areas of Somalia.
3. **Sustainable Agriculture Systems**: Developing and promoting sustainable agricultural production systems, rights of ownership and management of natural resources.

### Target Areas
The project will be implemented in listed bold areas:
- Southwest State, Lower Shabelle region: Afgoye, Barawe, Kurtunwaayo, and Qoryooley districts.
- Hiranbadele state, Middle Shabelle region: Cadale district.
- Jubaland state, Lower Juba region: Kismayo district.
- Puntland state, Togdheer region: Dhexe district.
- Somaliland state, Togdheer region: Oodweyn district.
- Galmudug State, Mudug region: Hobyo district.


---
**Page 112**
---

7. Women’s adaptive capacity and vulnerability to climate change in Somalia: Women in Somalia, especially those in rural areas, are most affected due to reliance on natural resources affected by climate for their livelihoods. They are at the frontline confronting the challenges of climate change but are poorly equipped and under-resourced to respond to the challenges. They have limited access to economic opportunities and decision-making power within households and communities. As a result, climate change increases the scarcity of resources and men migrate; women are pushed to assume new responsibilities in their absence. Women’s adaptive capacity is further constrained by lack of safe living spaces due to socio-cultural norms and gender-based discrimination. Conflict has ended the gains made in the area of women’s rights and empowerment, leading to increased vulnerability for women and girls.

8. The Somalia National Adaptation Plan of Action describes pastoralist communities, IDPs, and women as the most vulnerable groups to climate change due to lack access to information, markets, services, and technology. Women are particularly vulnerable because of their unequal division of labor, their unequal access to material and resources, non-material and their low participation in decision-making.

Objective and rationale for the gender assessment

9. Gender inequality is very high in Somalia, as various global gender inequality measurements indicate. This makes women more vulnerable to climate change and lowers the adaptation capacity of not only women but also their families, who depend on them and their communities. Therefore, it is important to understand how women experience the effects of climate change and what constraints they face in participating in and benefiting from the project, as well as how gender equality might prevent the project’s objectives from being achieved. The gender assessment will provide a better understanding of these issues and identify potential gender-based constraints the project needs to address and strategies that can be used to address them.

10. The objective of this gender assessment is to understand how gender relations in Somalia and the project districts, particularly affect the way men and women experience the problem of climate change and their ability to adapt to it. Based on the findings from the gender analysis and the main findings of the assessment, a gender action plan that can be implemented by the project to address identified gaps is developed, along with a list of indicators that can be used to measure progress in closing these gender gaps.

Part 2. Methodology

11. The methodology for the assessment included desk review, primary data collection through a survey administered to women and key informant interviews. Evaluated consultations have taken place in seven project target districts at community level: (i) in Odeleyne district, Togdheer region, Somaliland; (ii) in Cadale district, Lower Juba, Jubaland; (iii) in Galmudug state, Hargeisa region, Somaliland; (iv) in Garowe District, Nugaal region, Puntland; (v) in Eyl District, Nugal region, Puntland; (vi) in Garowe Districts, Nugal region, Puntland. Focus Group Discussions have been conducted at community level have been conducted with women and youth groups in the seven project target districts. Key informant interviews were conducted with Farmers' Cooperatives where women and youth were also represented.

12. Gender and climate change-related national policy documents, national gender profiles developed by FAO Somalia, and other documents like the last Demographic and Health Survey of Somalia were included in the desk review.

13. A telephone survey questionnaire was administered to 257 mostly women farmers in seven project districts: (i) in Odeleyne district, Togdheer region, Somaliland; (ii) in Cadale district, Lower Juba, Jubaland; (iii) in Galmudug state, Hargeisa region, Somaliland; (iv) in Eyl District, Nugal region, Puntland; (v) in Garowe Districts, Nugal region, Puntland. The survey represented different socio-economic livelihood and demographic categories.
- Participation in agro-postharvest livelihood and one of the following three major Value Chains: Sesame, Maize and Sorghum
- Representatives from women groups
- Representatives of different clans including minorities
- Age group (18–60 years old with young women making up 10% to 15% of the sample).

14. Focus Group informant interviews were conducted in Somaliland state with the Ministry of Employment, Social Affairs, and Family, the Ministry of Livestock, and a civil society organization called PEFNA, a women-led network that supports livelihood activities in Odeleyne district.

The key informant interview questionnaire was used to capture qualitative information on gender relations in agriculture, including the participation of men and women in various agricultural activities; their access to productive resources such as land, livestock, seeds, tools, and inputs; their decision-making over agricultural activities and income; and the distribution of responsibilities for unpaid core work.

16. The key informant interview questionnaire was used to capture more qualitative information about gender relations in agriculture, including the participation of men and women in various agricultural activities that they engage in and the kinds of challenges they face; how farmers are targeted for the provision of various inputs and services by government agencies and non-governmental organizations (NGOs); Farmers' organizations and barriers to participation in the organizations; and the needs of men and women farmers in livestock and crop agriculture.

Part 3\. Legal and Institutional Framework

### 3\.1 National gender policy framework and implications for the project

#### 17\. Protections that protect the rights of women were first included in the Federal Republic of Somalia's provisional constitution when it was formulated in 2012. The constitution recognized the need to ensure the implementation of laws, policies, and programs that promote equality between men and women, protect their social and economic rights, protect women from violence, and eliminate discrimination against women in all its forms. In 2013, the government adopted the National Development Plan (NDP) for the period 2013-2017. The New Deal framework developed in 2013 included commitments to advance gender disparity in various sectors.

#### 18\. The 9th National Development Plan of Somalia (2020-2024) had adopted strengthening gender, human rights and social equity as one of its cross-cutting policy objectives. The four pillars of the National Development Plan are: inclusive economic growth; sustainable peace and security; effective governance for inclusive economic growth and improved social development have each gender related targets and indicators. Under the framework of the 9th NDP, the government has committed to ensuring that women's rights councils and local government institutions are set as one target. Under the economic development pillar, investment in human capital is a key priority. The government also commits to ensuring that youth are prioritized. In order to diversify the economy, investment in skills training in TVEs targeting sectors with high employment potential for young people is a key priority. To ensure that women's development pillar national policies and strategies are developed to protect vulnerable groups. Among these are the development of the Somali Women's Charter, the FGM Act and the National Gender Policy.

#### 19\. Somalia has made good progress regarding Women Peace and Security (WPS) National Action Plans (NAPs). The NAP was adopted in 2015 by the Federal Government of Somalia. The MAP advanced by the government of Sweden through UN Women Somalia. The MAP advances a gender perspective on peacebuilding, security sector reform, and justice and law enforcement at all levels as a critical foundation for effective governance, sustainable peace, and development. In addition to the NAP, the government has also adopted other policies that address gender-based violence, access to justice, participation in transitional justice mechanisms, as well as climate change adaptation.

#### 20\. A National Gender Policy was adopted by the Federal Government of Somalia in 2016 to guide gender integration in its peace and state-building process. The objective of the gender policy is to create an enabling environment for women's full and equal participation in all spheres of life, including global opportunities. Specific policy objectives consist of eliminating all forms of gender-based discrimination and reducing the gap between men and women in access to resources and services. Promoting behavior change to shift gender norms and taking affirmative action as a corrective measure to equitable representation of women in decision-making processes. The National Gender Policy aligns with global frameworks like Convention on Elimination of All Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and introduces the concept of gender equality into all public policies, programs, and projects. Representation in political and leadership positions and track public budget allocation to gender equity objectives.

#### 21\. The national gender policy has four thematic priority areas: economic empowerment; health education and social protection; access to justice and participation in decision-making processes; and access to income sources, establishes a quota for women's employment in decision making positions in private and public sector entities. It also provides for the establishment of a National Women's Commission.

#### 22\. States such as Puntland and Somaliland also drafted their state gender policies. For example, the Somaliland National Gender Policy was developed in 2009 by the Ministry of employment and social


---
**Page 115**
---

affairs and family. The overall goal of the policy is to facilitate the mainstreaming of the needs and concerns of women and girls in all areas of sustainable development and poverty eradication. The policy has several priority areas covering different sectors; its specific objectives include elimination of inequality in access to resources, ensuring equal participation in decision making in social, economic, political and cultural spheres, promoting gender equality and empowerment and mainstreaming gender in all sectors of development. Under its poverty reduction and economic empowerment component, the policy aims at increasing women's income earning opportunities for her for women to participate in natural-resources management and support small scale entrepreneurs to create jobs and earn a living.

23. At the Federal level, the Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development (MWHRD) of the Federal Government is responsible for mainstreaming gender in all sectors and areas of development, including human rights, including the rights of women, children and other vulnerable groups. The Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development was established by the Federal government in 2013 to lead the development of national policies on gender equality and women's empowerment. National Gender Policies are developed with the participation of various stakeholders. The National Gender Policy is expected to be updated every five years for consultation and review of the implementation of the gender policy.

24. This Ministry also coordinates the implementation of the government's machineries. The Ministry of Family Affairs in Punjab is responsible for mainstreaming gender in different sectors, and in Sindh, it is the responsibility of the Department of Women and Child Development. The Ministry of Family (MESAF) is a mandate to serve specific target groups of the society, especially women, children, people with disabilities, and other socially disadvantaged groups including refugees, asylum seekers, etc., ensuring their access to education, health care, employment opportunities, and protection support are protected. MESAF is expected to set up multi-sectoral committees at all levels for the implementation of gender equality policies and programs and implementation of a multi-sectoral approach. Gender focal points are expected to be created in each government office and department. The National Gender Policy 2013 recognizes that successful implementation of the gender policy will be in a collaborative effort between government, NGOs and UN agencies and development partners.

25. However, despite the development of the Federal and state level National Gender Policies and the efforts, in individual development plans, the implementation leaves a lot to be desired.

National Climate Change Policy 2023 recognizes that gender equality is an important precondition for successful climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. The policy highlights the importance of mainstreaming gender into all national policies and programs, including those related to climate change. It stresses the importance of building the capacity of relevant institutions to mainstream gender in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts. The policy also recognizes that women are disproportionately affected by climate change and calls for their inclusion as key stakeholders in the design of climate funds. The policy commits to increasing the resilience of vulnerable groups including women, youth, and pastoralist communities at both the federal level and in the states of Punjab and Sindh.

The National Climate Change Policy 2013 recognized women, youth, and pastoralist communities as the most vulnerable to climate change. Women and youth are disproportionately affected by climate change and their inclusion is a key consideration for adaptation strategies. The policy commits to increasing the resilience of vulnerable groups including women, youth, and pastoralist communities at both the federal level and in the states of Punjab and Sindh. However, in the actual adaptation plans of both the federal government and the state of Sindh, there are no specific strategies

28. The National Determined Contributions (NDCs) of Somalia updated in 2021 recognizes that women are disproportionately affected by climate change and highlights the need to address gender equality and the impact of climate change disproportionately affects women because of their lack of access and control over resources, including land, water, and other productive assets. The NDC also emphasizes gender equality and the empowerment of women in climate change decision making. Specific gender targets developed under the NDC include increasing women’s representation in national and local government and climate change decision-making, making the Ministry of Gender and Human Rights part of the NDC governance structure, and ensuring that at least 50% of all projects funded by the NDC are implemented by women. The document also commits to undertaking gender analysis to inform climate adaptation and mitigation sector’s gender needs, to strengthening the of most vulnerable including women through social safety nets, and to increasing access for women to information on climate change and gender responsive technology. Finally, the document also highlights the need for developing insurance systems to enable women to better manage risks associated with climate change, particularly in pastoralist and support vulnerable groups, particularly women in climate change adaptation efforts.

29. Overall, Somalia has made significant progress towards gender equality and women’s empowerment. There is a strong political will to promote gender equality in climate change. However, there are gaps in the institutional framework for gender equality and women’s empowerment at all levels of government and civil society. The project will support the Ministry of Gender and Human Rights to build the capacity of national partners on gender-sensitive agriculture program delivery to ensure the projects’ success. The project will also support the Ministry of Gender and Human Rights to include further assessments and capacity building plans on gender for ministries of agriculture at federal, state, regional, and district levels.

3.2 Gender legal framework and implications for the project

30. Somalia has ratified both the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights and the International Covenant on Economic, Social, and Cultural Rights as of January 24, 1995. Article 3 in both covenants calls for states to ensure the equal rights of women and men in the enjoyment of the rights outlined in the conventions. The country has also ratified several other international instruments that protect the rights of women and girls. For example, Somalia has signed the Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) as well as the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights and the Rights and the Rights of Women in Africa. However, both conventions have not been ratified by the state to become law. The Ministry of Gender and Human Rights was established in 2018 under the rights established a CEDAW ratification and advocacy committee in 2019 as fast-track the process. However, ratification has yet to be completed.

31. Somalia has also signed and recognized the Beijing Declaration and the Platform for Action on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment, which is the first comprehensive and transformative global agenda for gender equality and women’s empowerment. The country has ratified 20 national laws that protect women’s rights under development. The Federal Government of Somalia has introduced a draft law to criminalize domestic violence and other forms of sexual offenses that are waiting for approval by the states. The federal government and both states have also introduced several other laws on gender equality, including the National Gender Equality Policy. At the federal government and Puntland state, a 30% quota for women’s representation in leadership positions has been instituted; while in Somaliland, a 25% quota is assigned.

32. The country has not yet ratified any of its international or national laws that protect rural women’s rights to own assets and access services are not yet in place in Somalia. For example, ratification of the CEDAW convention would have required states to implement Article 14 of the convention, which

# Part 4: Overview of Gender Inequalities in the Country

## 4.1 Poverty and demography

### 34. Women make up 46% of the population in Somalia, according to the 2014 population estimation survey carried out by the UNFPA and the Somali Ministry of Planning.
- **Women-headed households** make up 57% of all households in rural areas compared with only 29% of urban households. This is due to a high level of female-headed households, especially among nomadic communities.

### 35. Various global measures show the prevalence of a high level of gender inequality in Somalia.
- The Gender Inequality Index (GII) ranks Somalia at 140 out of 189 countries, placing the country as the fourth-lowest country for gender equality globally.
- The Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) ranks Somalia at 135 out of 136 countries, indicating that women are more disadvantaged than men in all areas.

### 36. Women's participation in the labor force and employment is lower than that of men.
- A 2018 UNFPA data shows that only 44% of men participate in the labor force compared to 57% for women, with a female employment rate of 44% vs. 63% for men.

### 37. Women's participation is lower than that of men.
- The employment rate is 14% for men and 27% for women. Reasons for women's low labor force participation include lack of education, limited access to credit, and a high level of unpaid care work.
- According to the 2017 World Bank report on women in agriculture, only 35% of women are employed in the agricultural sector.

### 38. Young women in Somalia have fewer livelihood opportunities than young men due to abysmal employment and earnings prospects.
- Only 40–42% of young people between the ages of 25 and 29 (men: 46% and women: 25%) are employed.

### 39. Young men in Somalia have better opportunities than young women due to higher levels of education, especially among young women.
- Young men face fewer difficulties securing livelihoods due to a lack of available jobs, poverty development, and family pressure to marry early or enter the military.
- Young women earn less than men because of lower levels of education, discrimination, and social and family pressures. They are also more likely to be forced into marriage or to engage in prostitution.

### 40. Young men can less land than older men due to ancestral land being less and with each sub-district, and existing portions are now almost too small to sub-divide.
- Young men are given few opportunities for land inheritance compared to older men.


---
**Page 118**
