39. Elderly people aged 60 and above made up 4.5% of the population in 2020. There is a lack of data on the situation of elderly people and their vulnerability to poverty in Somalia.

A study conducted by HelpAge International found that older women are more vulnerable than men, with 18% of older women living below the poverty line compared to 15% of those living in IDP settlements. The majority of the elderly people were women who were widowed or divorced and lived alone. The study also highlighted the need for increased support and support elderly people and address their needs. The study also found declining community support for older people, with many communities not providing adequate care to the elderly.

40. Women-headed households

41. Four in fifteen households in Somalia are headed by women. The proportion of woman-headed households is high in Mogadishu and other urban centers (52%) and (54%), compared to rural households (37%). There were also more female heads of household among IDP households than non-IDP households, who were divorced, separated, or widowed, and so female household heads, who are married but their husbands have died.

A World Bank survey in 2019 showed that the incidence of poverty is higher for male-headed households than for female-headed households in IDPs and non-displaced communities. The difference was most pronounced among IDP households with children (35%) compared to non- displaced communities (3%), while it is higher in IDPs (11–14%). Among the non-displaced community, women-headed households are more likely than male-headed households to be poor. Women caregivers experience higher poverty than IDP families without children. In IDP households, where there are more female heads of household and fewer male heads of household, poverty is higher.

The gender of the head of household to several other factors, like the number of children and the number of income earners in a household, have an impact on poverty levels. The study also found that the association between a household's demographic composition and its income profile shows women's lack of control over resources. Women-headed households are more likely than male-headed households to have an overall higher risk of poverty.

42. Other vulnerable groups

43. Poverty is highest in Somalia, with 7 out of 10 households living below the poverty line. However, some communities are more affected and deprived. Three-fourths of the population in rural areas, IDP settlements, and urban slums live on less than $2 per day, compared to two-thirds of people in non-poor communities.

44. Twenty-six percent of the population in Somalia lives in nomadic areas. Twenty-eight percent of nomadic households are headed by women. Women-headed households experience higher poverty; for example, IDP households are 10% more likely to be poor than non-IDP households.

45. Nomadic households have low access to education. The net enrolment rate among nomadic communities is 12% for primary and secondary education levels. Seventy-three percent of the people in nomadic communities live on less than $2 per day, compared with only 38% of those living in five people living in rural communities can read and write. Seventy-eight percent of nomadic male household members and eighty-four percent of female household members have no access to


---
**Page 119**
---

education. They have low access to health services, and most women receive no assistance from health professionals during childbirth.

47. Nomadic households have poor access to water supply and sanitation services. Only 35% of nomadic households have access to water, compared to 55% of rural households and 76% of urban households. A high proportion (80%) of nomadic households obtain their drinking water from unsafe sources, such as rivers or wells, which are more likely to be contaminated with bacteria than other sources. Only 12% of nomadic households have access to improved sanitation facilities compared to 25% of rural and 23% of urban households. Less than 1% of nomadic households use electricity for lighting.

### 4.2 Health

The health system in Somalia is fragmented and under-resourced to meet the healthcare needs of the population. Various health measurements indicate the general low health status of the population, including high infant mortality rates and poor maternal health indicators. The life expectancy at birth is one of the lowest in the world.

### 48. The health system in Somalia is fragmented and under-resourced to meet the healthcare needs of the population. Various health measurements indicate the general low health status of the population, including high infant mortality rates and poor maternal health indicators. The life expectancy at birth is one of the lowest in the world. Only 32% of births are assisted by a healthcare provider and 31% of mothers receive antenatal care. The main obstacles for women to access health services include lack of money, distance from health facilities, and fear of being stigmatized or discriminated against (42%). Poor women and women living in nomadic communities are most affected by a lack of health services.

### 49. Women and children are affected by poor nutrition and the prevalence of diseases. Twenty-eight percent of children under five years old suffer from acute malnutrition, with 10% suffering from wasting. Twenty-six percent of women in nomadic communities and 16% in rural communities are underweight. Diseases such as malaria, tuberculosis, and diarrhea are common due to high use of firewood and shrubs for cooking in households and diarrhea affects the children most.

### 50. The prevalence of HIV/AIDS is low in Somalia (0.5%). Sixty-six percent of women aged 15–49 have heard about AIDS, but only 38% know that it can be transmitted through sexual intercourse. Only one-third of women who are sexually active use a method to prevent pregnancy. More than half of the population in Somalia has access to improved drinking water and sanitation facilities.

### 51. Early marriage and teen pregnancy are prevalent in Somalia. The 2020 DHS found that 16% of ever-married women aged 15–49 were married before age 18, with a higher prevalence among rural women (37%). Seventy percent of women between the ages of 15 and 19 have given birth or are pregnant with their first child. Teen pregnancy is more common in nomadic communities than urban areas.

### 52. Despite high levels of knowledge about modern contraception methods, only one-third of women aged 15–49 know about modern contraception methods. Only a quarter use any method of contraception (7%). There is a high level of unmet need for contraception (37%) in rural and nomadic areas.

### 53. More than half of the population in Somalia has access to improved drinking water and sanitation facilities, but there are significant disparities between urban, rural, and nomadic communities. Access to safe drinking water is low for nomadic communities (34%) and rural communities (30%). Forty-five percent of nomadic households obtain their drinking water from unsafe sources such as rivers or wells, which are more likely to be contaminated with bacteria than other sources. Only 12% of nomadic households have access to improved sanitation facilities compared to 25% of rural and 23% of urban households. Less than 1% of nomadic households use electricity for lighting.

### 4.3. Literacy and Education

#### 4.3.1. Educational Attainment among Women in Somalia

53. Educational attainment among women is very low. Overall, 72% of women aged 15–49 have not attended school at all. Twenty percent of women have some level of primary education, but only 3% completed primary schooling. Moreover, 5% of women attended secondary school, but only 2% completed secondary education. Three percent of women have completed higher levels of education.

#### 4.3.2. Literacy among Women Aged 15–49 by Place of Residence

54. Literacy among women aged 15–49 varies by place of residence. Among women residing in urban areas, the literacy rate is 60%, compared to 78% among those living in rural areas and 34% among women living in nomadic areas.

### 4.4. Voice and Representation

#### 4.4.1. Patriarchy and Women's Political Participation

55. A patriarchal culture prevails in Somalia, and women are often excluded from political decision-making at all levels of community levels. At the national level, women hold 25% of the seats in the federal parliament through a quota system. In the states of Puntland and Somaliland, women hold only 10% of the parliamentary seats. Women hold no ministerial positions in the federal government; however, while in Somaliland, women make up only 4% of the cabinet positions, and in Puntland, only 1 out of 16 cabinet positions is held by a woman.

#### 4.4.2. Women's Limited Representation in Leadership and National Decision-Making Positions

56. Women’s limited representation in leadership and national decision-making positions is one of the reasons for the slow improvement of gender commitments included in the national gender policy and development plans, as well as for the lack of impact of interventions on women’s empowerment and rights of women. There is high resistance from traditional clan and religious leaders to implementing the political office quota system. Women are also underrepresented in local government structures, which rely on clan-based committees that are entirely composed of men, excluding women.

#### 4.4.3. Women's Limited Access to Financial Services

57. Women’s limited access to financial services is a major barrier to their empowerment. According to the 2020 DHS, 90% and 60% of women, respectively, reported that they take part in decisions about how to spend the cash they and their husbands earn from work or business activities. However, only 15% and 34%, respectively, make household purchases. A significant proportion of women respondents also said decisions on their health care and mobility are made by their spouses. Forty-five percent of women said their spouses decide whether they should use a cash card, and 46% said their spouses decide whether they can visit family members and relatives.

### 4.5. Entrepreneurship

#### 4.5.1. Women's Overrepresentation in the Informal Sector

58. In the economic sector, women are overrepresented in the informal sector. Women’s entrepreneurship is constrained by low levels of literacy and access to support services and networks. Women have limited access to financial literacy, vocational, and business skills training. They have low access to formal credit institutions and microfinance schemes. Only 10% of women entrepreneurs in Somalia were fully commissioned by FAO and UNDP in sozimbo, maize and sesame value chains in Somalia showed that over 95% of the women respondents had no access to any form of financial services.

#### 4.5.2. Women's Limited Mobility Due to Unpaid Care and Domestic Work

59. Women’s limited mobility due to shortage of time because of their unpaid care and domestic work

### Access to and control over resources

#### Women’s role in livestock production
Women play a key role in both livestock and crop agriculture activities. They are responsible for the production of smaller livestock, dairy processing, and marketing, while men are responsible for the herding, slaughtering, and meat processing.

#### Women's role in land ownership
Women have limited access to control over land and other resources. In rural areas, women own 10% of all land, respectively, jointly with their spouses or alone. Older women are more likely to have ownership of land and a house compared to younger women. For example, 62 percent of women aged 45-49 years own land, as opposed to only 38 percent of women under the age of 15.

#### Women's access to land
Women’s access to land is curtailed by discriminatory customary practices. Customary laws do not consider women as equal members of their communities and therefore do not allow them to inherit or own land; but they can have access rights through their husbands or brothers. However, they can easily lose their access to land when the husband dies because it is considered a gift from his family.

#### Women's role in livestock production
Women are responsible for smaller livestock such as goats, sheep, and chickens, while men have control over larger cattle and camels. The formal ownership rights of all livestock lie with the male head of household. The marketing of livestock is usually controlled by men who decide on the income earned from the sale.

#### Women's access to information
Women’s access to agriculture extension services is limited due to time and mobility constraints as a result of unpaid care work responsibilities and social norms. Women often do not participate in technical and vocational training programs, which are essential for improving their agricultural skills. Education and literacy among women are also a constraint on their participation. Women are not able to access veterinary services offered by the government, such as livestock vaccination services. Agriculture extension services provided through radio and television are also limited because of low literacy rates. It is considered socially unacceptable for women to move around without supervision.

#### Media use
Nowadays, most rural households have access to media sources, including newspapers, radio, and TV at least once a week. Media access is especially low among the poorest category of women in rural areas. The most common source of information used by women in urban areas is television, while for the poorest households, radio is the most frequently used source of information. The use of


---
**Page 122**
---

The internet is very low. Only 17% of women have ever used the internet. Among rural women, only 12% have ever used the Internet, while in remote communities, only 1% have used the internet before.

Mobile ownership is high among women with 75% of women owning a mobile phone. Mobile ownership is slightly less among women with no education (72%) compared to women with higher education (89%). In urban areas, 60 percent of rural women and 56% of women living in remote communities own a mobile phone.

### Access to Finance

60. Women's access to and control over credit is limited. A study commissioned by FAO and UNDP in several Somali regions found that only 12% of women had ever borrowed money from any source, reflecting the in expression studied lack of finance. The reason for lack of finance for 44% was their inability to qualify for loans because they were not considered creditworthy. Another 30% said they did not borrow because banks require a male guarantor for 25%. Of the respondents and lack of security to access loans (18%). Women who had borrowed money from friends or family (16%) said that they were allowed to take loan and another 16% said carrying the amount money women can access from banks was a problem.

66. Only 4% of women in Somalia have bank accounts, according to the 2020 DHS. Although very few women own bank cards, the introduction of mobile money has enabled women to have a level of financial independence that they did not have before and use mobile phones for financial transactions.

### Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH)

Conflict and displacement have heightened the risk of sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) against women and girls. Men due to the conflict or migrate from home, leaving women to assume household responsibilities. Women who are displaced, especially those who are married to members of extremist groups, are displaced due to the loss of economic opportunities. Women and children make up 70% of internally displaced persons (IDPs) in Somalia. The lack of education, poor health, social breakdown, and limited leadership and control. Single women and women from minority groups are especially vulnerable to SGBV. The Somali government has made some progress on the issue of violence in SGBV cases except men and boys from facing prosecution, and there is a tendency to stigmatize and shame women who have been raped. Women in IDP sites have little access to formal legal recourse.

### Other Forms of GBV

4.10. Spousal violence is prevalent in Somalia. In the 2020 DHS, 12% of ever-married women reported that they had been abused physically by a spouse, while one percent reported emotional abuse by a spouse. The prevalence was highest among women aged 35–49 years (17%) and lowest for women aged 60+ years and Normadic areas (11%). Few women (17%) out of those who experienced spousal violence have sought help. This is because of the social stigma attached to domestic violence, lack of awareness about the number of reasons. The attitude that justifies spousal violence is especially high (44%) among women in poorer households.

69. FGM is highly prevalent in Somalia. Among women aged 15–49 years, 99 percent have been circumcised. Out of these, 64% have gone through pharaonic circumcision, which is the most severe form of circumcision. The practice has been declining over time; however, a 2020 DHS said they want the practice to continue, while 72% believe that it is a religious obligation.

Part 5. Gender Inequalities Exacerbated by Climate Change

70. The Somalia National Adaptation Plan of Action describes pastoralist communities, IDPs, and women as the most vulnerable groups in Somalia. These communities must face multiple climate risks and impacts, vulnerably of individuals varies depending on their adaptive capacity which is influenced by their gender, geography, types of livelihoods, and age.

71. Pastoralists are often nomadic or semi-nomadic and have very few fixed assets, which makes them vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. People living in IDP camps due to conflict or drought face similar challenges as they lack access to land, water, and other resources that are critical for their survival.

72. Women play a vital role in the management of natural resources and are often the most affected when negative impacts of climate change occur. They are responsible for collecting firewood, water, and food from natural systems, with devastating impacts on women's livelihoods, household poverty, and agricultural productivity. Women also face increased vulnerability given their lack equal rights of ownership and control over land, property and other productive assets compared to their male counterparts. This is especially true in developing countries where women are often excluded from decision-making processes.

73. During a drought, when men migrate in search of employment, women are forced to become heads of households. In addition to the increased responsibility for household management and childcare, women must find solutions to feed their families. They must walk long distances - 5-8 km at times - to get water from distant sources, increasing vulnerability for households use and food security risks as they travel in search of livelihoods. When communities are forced into migration due to drought, conflicts and disasters, women are not represented.

74. FAD (2014) found that the occurrence of extreme weather events would reduce the income of female-headed households significantly more than those of male-headed households. Due to economic vulnerability, female-headed households have less opportunities to compensate on-farm income loss by off-farm earnings compared to their male counterparts.

75. Women play a key role for food security in Somalia. Women are forced to adopt negative coping strategies when food is limited during droughts due to limited resources. They cope by limiting food consumption, reducing the number of meals per day and cutting down on other essential expenditures such as buying medicine or paying school fees.

76. Climate change would increase the vulnerability and exposure of women and girls to GBV and SEAH. Young girls are forced to go into commercial sex work for survival in the event of droughts. Women are also increasingly vulnerable to sexual violence, including rape, harassment, and trafficking during migration.

77. Household frequency and intensity of extreme weather events could increase the risk of families undertaking early marriage for their female children. As families are anxious to secure economic security in case of extreme weather events, there has been an increased trend in cases of FGM to ensure marriageability of their female children.

Part 6. Gender issues in the project implementation sites

### 6.1. Gender profile of the implementation sites

77. The project implementation sites are located in six states of Somalia, namely, South West, Hiranbelle, Puntland, Galgudug, Jubaland, and Somaliland. Most of the households in the implementation districts can be categorized as poor (74%), while about 25% are categorized as being in the middle wealth group.

### 78. The main sources of income for households in the project sites are farming (51%), followed by livestock sales and cash assistance (28%), and humanitarian cash support (14%). Very few households (7%) rely on wage employment, while only a small number (3%) depend on fishing or hunting. Other sources include sorghum, cowpeas, mug beans, and vegetables. Fodder production is also common in Somaliland.

### 79. Women are responsible for most of the agricultural activities such as planting, weeding, harvesting, and collecting; men are responsible for sowing crops or irrigation. In livestock production, women are responsible for the production of small livestock like goats and sheep and dairy processing. Men are responsible for purchasing feed for livestock and selling large quantities of livestock.

### 6.2. Gender gaps identified

The project sites in this study are agro-pastoral areas where households rely both on crop and livestock production for their livelihoods. Most farm decisions, such as the type of seed varieties to cultivate, farm inputs (fertilizers, pesticides), and livestock breeds, are made by men. According to survey responses, these decisions are made by men alone in 50% of the cases or in consultation with women in their households in 30% of the cases. In the survey, women made decisions alone in 12% of all cases.

82. Survey respondents in the project sites mentioned that women are reached by agricultural extension and training programs to a limited extent. Women’s access to credit is also limited due to time and mobility constraints as well as unpaid care work responsibilities at home and local norms. A measurement carried out with key informants in Somaliland also indicated that women's time is limited by unpaid care


---
**Page 125**
---

83. Access to credit is limited for both men and women in the project sites. The most common sources of credit are informal loans from friends or family members, microfinance institutions (MFIs), banks, and mobile financial transactions are common. There is also an emergence of village-level saving and loan associations organized with the support of NGOs. Women are engaged in VSLA and save to pool small funds.

84. VSLA (Village Saving and Loan Association) is a self-help group of women who come together into groups, usually 10-25 members, to save money on a weekly basis. The savings are pooled by the groups and are trained on the VSLA methodology and life skills. The groups have five management committees and a public officer. The funds can be used for any purpose that is agreed upon by all group members. This would give women the opportunity to access loans that can be used to start or improve an existing business, buy livestock, pay school fees, or other expenses. However, this type of credit requires documentation. FAO works towards linking these VSLA groups to formal financial services and this would give women the opportunity to access ample amount of loans that can be used to improve and diversify their income.

85. Both men and women engage in the marketing of agricultural products. However, their level of engagement varies significantly. Women are more likely than men to sell produce at local market or export markets, while women invest livestock products such as meat and dairy in local markets. Income from the sale of milk is controlled by men; however, women can derive income earned from the sale of dairy and meat.

86. Key informants interviewed indicated that farmers in the project sites have needs for training on livestock production techniques, support to improve their access to inputs such as vaccines/services, fodder, feed, and water for livestock. Women also indicated the need to improve their current level of income from livestock production. Men indicated a need for access to fodder, feed, and water.

### 63. Barriers

78. The barriers to gender equality in agriculture in the target areas are socio-cultural norms and capacity gaps at both national and policy levels.

79. Socio-cultural norms influence various aspects of life that contribute to limiting women's roles, opportunities, and the benefits they can enjoy in agriculture. Gender stereotypes are designed almost all agricultural extension services. Women are expected to engage in productive work outside agricultural trainings and meetings, participate in political activities, or spend time on their businesses. These expectations often conflict with the responsibilities of women for household chores, inputs, and market. Social norms influence who makes decisions in the household on agricultural input and sale of produce. In most cases, men make these decisions without consulting with women. Women are also expected to be responsible for child care and other domestic work, which impedes their engagement in economic activities to mobility and their health, is curtailed by social norms.

80. Gender stereotypes are designed almost all agricultural extension advisory services. Various studies show women's access to agricultural extension is improved when there are female extension agents. Literature review and key informant interviews in the project districts show that women prefer female extension officers because they can relate better with them, and it is inappropriate for women to move from place to place without supervision.

81. At times, women face restrictions on their movement due to cultural norms or lack of services to provide gender-sensitive services. Extension services don't take into account women's mobility restrictions or unpaid care work responsibilities. There is limited entrepreneurship training support for women engaged in the

# 6.4 Gender roles and barriers in maize, sesame, sorghum and fodder value chains

## 6.4.1 Sorghum value chain

### 6.4.2 Maize value chains

51 FAO 2024. Sorghum, Maize and Sesame value chains in Somalia. Scala private sector engagement facility report.


---
**Page 127**
---

99. Both men and women participate in maize value chains in production and marketing. Men are responsible for the purchase and application of inputs such as fertilizers and pesticides. Both men and women participate in planting, irrigating, weeding, and harvesting.

100. Women are involved in trading maize, selling small volumes of maize in local markets. They also sell maize stalks for fodder. Men are involved in transporting large quantities of maize to markets to sell to commercial traders.[52]

### 6.4.3. Sesame value chain

101. Sesame is a commercial crop in the project districts. Smallholder producers and commercial farmers dominate sesame production. Most of the producers are smallholders with 1–2 hectares of land. Commercial production accounts for only 20% of the total sesame production. Sesame is produced in both irrigation and rain-fed systems.

102. Village women buy from small producers and aggregate and sell to whole sellers, oil millers, and exporters. Oil mills are located in major towns in the capital city of Mogadishu, with a few processing facilities also located in towns like Baidoa and Jowhar. Retailers serve as links between processors and local consumers. Animal feed is produced from sesame meal and seedcake that can be used as animal feed.

103. Women play a key role in the sesame value chain women make up 70% of the work force in the sesame sector. At the production stage, while land preparation and planting are done by men, most other activities are undertaken by women and youth. Women are engaged in processing, cleaning, sorting, and drying of seeds to facilitate small-scale sesame trading and oil processing operations.

104. There are projects that support women in increasing their participation in good farming practices in sesame production through farmer field schools and demonstration plots as well as hiring female extension agents to reach women farmers.[53]

### 6.4.4. Fodder Value chain

105. The fodder value chain in the project districts includes fresh cut green grass, maize, and sorghum chopped and fed to animals, as well as whole dry sorghum stalks stacked, milled, and fed to livestock.

106. Both men and women participate in the fodder value chain. Men are responsible for planting sorghum and other crops that can be used as animal feed. Women are responsible for harvesting, cleaning, and stacking sorghum stover. Women are responsible for preserving seed for the new season, preparing feed for livestock, and feeding animals. Women also collect grasses and cut green maize fodder, and, together with men, transporting sorghum stovers on a donkey to markets. Organized women groups have been established in some districts as a result of this project to use as green or dry fodder and engaging in fodder processing such as cutting, adding grain, and packing.

107. Women are also fully responsible for feeding and fattening livestock and milk production and marketing. However, their control over livestock is limited. Women usually own only goats and sheep that have been given by family members. Men generally have more control over the money from all milk production. They particularly control sheep and goats.[54]

[52] FAO 2024. Sorghum, Maize and Sesame value chains in Somalia. Scala private sector engagement facility report. [53] FAO 2024. Gender baseline assessment report for the Somali Information and Resilience Building Action Phase II.

6.4.5. Challenges and gender-related barriers in the value chains

108\. The challenges smallholder farmers face in the production of sesame and fodder value chains are similar to those faced by other smallholder producers, such as improved seed varieties, low use of fertilizers, and mechanization services, is a challenge for smallholders. It contributes to low productivity.

109\. The provision of adequate extension and research services to support farmers in making decisions on the selection of appropriate seeds and farming techniques is another challenge. Access to credit for smallholders is also limited. Micro-loans provide credit to farmers, sometimes accepting surplus grain as profit.

110\. Lack of access to market information and market opportunities is a challenge for women cooperatives engaged in sesame and fodder production. A project on promoting inclusive markets in Somalia has been implemented by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) with an information system that provides real-time data on markets, prices, demand, and supply.

111\. Women participating in the value chains face additional barriers due to gender relationships. For example, women are often excluded from land ownership or inheritance rights resulting from socio-cultural norms and a lack of legal systems that protect their right to land. Land is passed through paternal lines, which means that women have no access to land. Women are also responsible for childcare, cooking together with male relatives and grown-up children. Women can be allocated land to grow fodder and vegetables by their husbands.

112\. Women face challenges accessing extension services due to their unpaid care work responsibilities, which limit the time they have to participate in agricultural trainings and meetings.

113\. Although both men and women farmers lack access to credit, women's access is further constrained. Women are unable to access credit, such as loans or micro-loans from banks. Socio-cultural norms do not allow women to take out loans from financial institutions. Banks ask women who apply for loans to prove a male guarantor or cap the amount of loan women can access. Banks consider women borrowers to be at high risk. Poor literacy levels also prohibit women from borrowing.

6.5\. Intersectional nature of gender inequality

114\. While the gender assessment showed women as a group have limited access to assets and resources, livelihood opportunities, and decision-making power as a result of gender relationships, it also indicated that other factors such as age, marital status, education level, employment status, and wealth status contribute to the social and economic empowerment and wellbeing outcomes of individuals. These factors interact with gender inequality in complex ways. They are influenced by age, wealth, geographic location, and household composition. These other forms of inequality, however, interact with gender inequality to create multiple layers of vulnerability and deeper levels of deprivation for women.

115\. In the project districts and in the country at large, most households are poor and live below or close to poverty lines. However, men and women living in rural areas, IDPs, and nomadic communities experience different levels of deprivation. Women living in urban areas with access to services have the highest level of deprivation, with low access to education, health, water supply and sanitation, electricity, and other basic needs.

116\. Age is a factor affecting the economic and social wellbeing of individuals, with young and elderly people experiencing discrimination and inequality. Young people have lower access to land, livestock, and other productive assets than older people. Women in rural areas are more vulnerable because women are even more vulnerable than that of young men because, in addition to age, gender-based socio-cultural


---
**Page 129**
---

117. Household size and composition affect households' vulnerability to poverty. Female-headed households are more vulnerable than male-headed households.

### Part 7. Strategies to integrate gender into the project

#### 7.1 Formulation and implementation

118. The gender action plan in this section is developed by analyzing how the gender relationships described above will affect the way men and women can participate and benefit from the project interventions. This analysis begins with an understanding of the linkages between the objectives of the project under each of the project components. This is followed by strategies and actions to ensure that all project activities are gender-sensitive.

119. The gender action plan takes account of the deeper levels of inequality and deprivation experienced by women in IDPs, including their lack of access to resources such as land, water, and income-generating opportunities; as well as women-headed households with larger number of children and lower number of economically active members in the household. The gender action plan also recognizes the limited livelihood opportunities and discrimination faced by women in IDP settings compared to men.

120. Under component one, the project aims to establish participatory landscape and natural resources management systems that are inclusive of all stakeholders. If these systems do not address women's mobility and representation in the public sphere, women are likely to be excluded from the consultation process and management committees. Young people, especially young women are also likely to be excluded due to their lack of access to information and decision-making processes.

121. The gender action plan will address the barrier to women's participation by organizing community dialogues targeting clan and traditional leaders, as well as youth and women groups. These dialogues will be organized with local committees and in consultations to design landscape management plans. The project will also set quotas for representation of women on all committees and ensure that at least 30% of the positions are held by women at various levels as well as for proportion of women who will participate in landscape management


---

# Component two of the project aims to develop the resilience of local livelihoods to climate change by improving access to agricultural extension services and supporting farmers to develop climate-resilient agriculture practices.

## The project will address these barriers in the following ways:

* **The project set a quota to increase the representation of women in WULACs and train women committee members on scheme management and gender equality.** This will ensure that women are better represented at all levels, including young people, especially young women.
* **Training and supporting women to engage in traditionally male-dominated agricultural extension services.** This will help to overcome the barriers faced by women who have not been trained before.
* **The trained women can also become role models for other women in the community.**
* **To ensure women's access to advisory services, a gender-sensitive rural advisory service system is being developed using a tool developed by FAO, Gender Sensitive Rural Advisory Services Assessment Tool (GRASAT).**

## Following the assessment, capacity-building training on how to develop and deliver gender-sensitive extension services will be provided. The training will be conducted at various levels.

* **The topics that will be covered in the training include: basic gender concepts and why gender equality is important; gender analysis of agricultural extension services; gender analysis of women's participation in agriculture production and the gaps in access to advisory services; gender division of labor in agriculture and extension advisory services using different scenarios as examples on how extension agents can raise awareness among farmers about gender issues.**

## The training will be conducted by a team of national-level and rural and state agricultural department staff on a TOT of gender-sensitive advisory services.

* **The training will be conducted in each of the six project states, with the first two states being targeted for collaboration with national agriculture extension organizations that have received the TOT.** The results of the training and changes to the knowledge and practice of extension agents will be monitored using the action plans developed at the end of the training.

end of the training. The training will also have a KAP assessment that will be administered at the beginning and end of the training.

126. Under component 2, the project will support women candidates for being elected as board members of WUCAs through capacity building for the WUCAs including raising awareness of the importance of women in water management and their role in decision-making processes. Under Activity 2.1.2, the project will rehabilitate and restore irrigation and water supply infrastructures based on a participatory approach involving all stakeholders. The project will also develop frameworks for equitable access to water and conflict prevention mechanisms. In addition, the project will deploy water-saving technologies such as drip irrigation systems, sprinkler irrigation systems, and micro-irrigation systems that provide farmers with increased use of water. In this activity, it is important to ensure that the water infrastructure constructed and rehabilitated is also informed by the water use needs of men and women in order to avoid gender-based conflicts over access to water resources. Under the responsibility of securing water for cropping and for livestock. The training on water-saving technologies will be provided to farmers who are members of WUCAs, and women farmers will be supported to access drip irrigation kits and solar operating pumping systems organized in farmer groups.

127. The project will undertake several activities to ensure the benefit of women from value chain development projects. These include providing women with training on business management skills to understand sources of seed for women and men, their ability to pay, and storage capacity to develop a subsidy system that takes account of the needs of both men and women. Women’s groups engaged in a specific project will be provided with training on how to manage their own resources within the business project. The project will also identify value chains where women are overrepresented and have a potential for growth. These include horticulture, livestock, and fisheries. The project will provide skills development and investment readiness, linking them with financing institutions and markets. In targeting women’s groups in these value chains, the project will focus on the empowerment of women who are vulnerable to poverty, such as single women heads of households with large family size and limited access to land or credit facilities. The project will also target young women heads of households with limited ownership of assets. The targeting will also focus on young people, especially youth women.

128. The success of women entrepreneurs supported by the project will be measured through indicators such as the number of women entrepreneurs who have been able to enter into new and more lucrative value chains, the number of women entrepreneurs who have increased their production capacity and increase their sales and income.

129. The project will also provide institutional tools to influence the development/improvement of financial products that are suitable for women agripreneurs, such as non-collateral-based credit systems, for example, microfinance institutions. In addition, the project will support women’s groups in order to develop business plans using FAO’s Rural Investment Tool. B2B meetings will be organized between women business owners and financial institutions to help them present their business plans and identify funding opportunities. The project will also provide training on how to manage their own resources and provide them with small grants to register their businesses, carry out audits, and fulfill other requirements for accessing credit facilities. In addition, the project will provide technical assistance for support attention will be paid to include young women and single women heads of households depending on the needs of each country.

130. Under activity 2.3.4, the project will support the rehabilitation of market infrastructure, including irrigation systems, roads, bridges, and other facilities that are critical in accessing markets and services. Furthermore, the project also creates new market poles to promote local exchanges and increase economic activity among women agripreneurs. Given that women play a significant role in agriculture jobs, especially during the off-seasons, it will be important to ensure the provision of continuous access for construction of market infrastructure, such as rural roads and district- and village-level regrouping points for small farmers. The project will also provide training on how to manage their own resources and business facilities and measures to ensure safety and security in the market.

131. Concluding the review of the project, it is recommended that an institutional framework be established for sustainable landscape management and climate-resilient agriculture at the state and federal levels.

One of the outputs to realize this outcome is increasing farmer and livestock producers' access to climate services. Although a significant proportion of women in the project sites have mobile phones, most women don't access the Internet. There is no evidence of the use of mobile devices to access agricultural information or extension services. 7.2. Monitoring, evaluation, and reporting

135. The project will collect sex-disaggregated data throughout the log frame where relevant. Gender-sensitive indicators for gender equality in education will be used to monitor and measure the progress of outputs included in the gender action plan. The section below provides a list of gender indicators included under each component.

136. Under component one, the project will collect data on the proportion of women and young people who are included in landscape management committees. The project will also monitor the number of dialogue meetings organized with women and youth entrepreneurs to discuss climate-resilient practices and to sensitize people about the importance of including women and young people in consultation processes during the development of landscape management plans. The project will collect data on whether they are represented in community structures established for landscape and natural resources management.

137. Under component two, the project will collect quantitative data on the proportion of women and youth entrepreneurs who have access to climate-resilient inputs and services provided by financial institutions or other service providers. Data will also be collected on the number of trainings organized and the number of women and youth entrepreneurs trained in climate-resilience practices.

138. On the development of climate-resilient value chains, the project will collect data on the number of women and youth entrepreneurs who have access to inputs and services provided by financial institutions or other service providers. The project will also collect data on the number of women and youth entrepreneurs able to enter into new and more lucrative value chains and the number of women and youth entrepreneurs who are able to sell their produce at a higher price than before.

139. Under component three, the project will collect data on the number of dialogue meetings organized between financial institutions or other service providers and women and youth entrepreneurs. The project will also collect data on the number of women-led businesses and businesses owned by young people that are supported to develop business plans, and the number of women-led businesses and businesses owned by young people that are able to access credit facilities and other sensitive financial products and services developed by financial institutions engaged by the project.

140. Under component three, the project will monitor the proportion of women users out of the total users of digital platforms for climate-resilience practices. The number of dialogue meetings organized will also be conducted throughout the project, which will help to capture qualitative information.

143. Under output 3.1, improved legal frameworks and implementation modalities on natural resource management will be used as a tool to improve women’s access to land and resources for sustainable land management to identify gaps. Following the review, policy dialogues will be conducted to address these gaps in order to ensure that women have equal opportunities to participate in sustainable land management and to improve coordination between different sectors.

140. There will also be an annual review of progress made in mainstreaming gender in the project implementation. The review will also be used as an opportunity to bring implementing partners together and share experiences on what is working well and what needs to be improved.


7.3. Mitigation of the risks related to SEAH and GBV

141. The project will be guided by FAO’s guidelines on gender equality and the prevention of gender-based violence, which are included in the organization’s Framework for Environmental and Social Management (ESM). The ESM is designed to prevent and mitigate adverse effects of any FAO-supported activity that may lead to or result from GBV/SEAH. It aims also to prevent and minimize the risk of GBV/SEAH occurring as a direct consequence of an FAO-supported project, reduce tension between men and women, and ensure proper and timely referral to relevant services for cases of discrimination, harassment, violence, abuse, and exploitation against women and girls in connection with any FAO-supported activity.

142. The project will also engage all stakeholders participating in the project about the links between GBV/SEAH and food insecurity in the context of the project in Somalia and opportunities for FAO to prevent GBV/SEAH and contribute to the protection of vulnerable individuals. The project will


---
**Page 134**
---

build the capacity of staff and other project stakeholders on the rights of serviced communities and the responsibilities of FAO personnel, implementing partners, and donors. It will also build their capacity on the guiding principles outlined below, which will be implemented by the project.

143. The guiding principles that will be implemented by the project to address the risks of gender-based violence include: (1) Ensuring all program activities are safe for women and girls; (2) Ensure safety and dignity of all people and avoid causing harm; (3) Ensure inclusive access paying attention to the needs of vulnerable groups, including women, children, persons with disabilities, and subject; (4) Ensuring participation and empowerment of men and women by communicating the program activities in a way that is accessible for all; (5) Ensuring accountability and transparency through close collaboration and partnership with organizations with GEV/SEAH expertise within the project areas; (6) Ensuring accountability to communities and beneficiaries to receive feedback on adequacy of interventions and to understand and address concerns.

144. The project will also adhere to a zero-tolerance policy for sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment. The project will adopt victim-centered approach, it comprises prevention and referral services.


### 145. FAO Somalia has a dedicated unit implementing the SMS-based Coordinated Complaints and Feedback System (CoCo) in order to ensure accountability of the Feedback to human resources contracts and associations policies. CoCo is a system that integrates and manages the data on complaints, compliance and feedback gathered through various channels such as: hotline calls, text messages, email, web forms, surveys (e.g., FAO surveys, emails), etc. The CoCo system enables FAO to respond to communities concerns systematically and in a timely manner. It also ensures that all complaints are recorded, tracked, analyzed and responsive to vulnerable people, including women, people with disabilities and other minority groups.

FAO Somalia has established a toll-free hotline number (079-214568) for reporting cases of sexual exploitation and abuse (SEAPSEA). Beneficiaries are made aware of FAO's toll-free Hotline number, operating on 30-hours basis, that they can use to report any case of sexual exploitation and report cases of fraud, diversion, sexual exploitation, and abuse. Beneficiaries can also lodge complaints or give feedback regarding FAO activities through the following channels: SMS, email, web forms, and hotline calls. The system is integrated in FAO's E-Platform. Hotline is communicated via several means such as during sensitization and communication events, community meetings, awareness raising campaigns, and through mobile phone SMS Voice messages. FAO's Hotline is in a FAO Hargeisa Field office and is handled by two FAO staff members who are trained to handle complaints and provide necessary support to the callers. The hotline covers all the callers and covers the entire Somalia thus allowing the most vulnerable to call without cost.

147. Awareness/radio campaigns are launched to accompany major activities to inform the population at large about the importance of reporting cases of sexual exploitation, abuse, fraud, diversion, and other forms in FAO projects. Bulk SMS (text and voice) are sent systematically to beneficiaries before activity starts to provide them with information on how to report cases of sexual exploitation, abuse, fraud, and diversion specific to PSEAPGBV to encourage beneficiaries and communities to report incidents to FAO and provide necessary support.


### 148. FAO Somalia established clear reporting channels and response mechanisms for beneficiaries and community members and favors the direct contact with beneficiaries via the Call Centre. FAO's toll-free hotline, SMS, email, web forms, and hotline calls are available as a means of communication to citizens via e-mails, implementing partners, district authorities, other platforms managed by other organizations, etc.


### 149. The GIRM will be survivor-centered and gender-responsive and will have specific procedures for SEAPGBV cases. FAO Somalia has established clear guidelines on how to report cases of sexual abuse. CoCo is accessible, inclusive, and survivor-centered. Clear information on how to access the GIRM will be widely disseminated through various channels such as awareness raising campaigns, community meetings, and project stakeholders. Training and awareness-raising sessions will be conducted to ensure all personnel involved in the grievance process understand the importance of a survivor-centered and gender-responsive approach. The GIRM will have a 10-day period for acknowledgment of receipt of a grievance and a 30-day resolution period.
